Formal category theory in ∞ \infty -equipments I
Jaco Ruit

Abstract
. We introduce a generalization of proarrow equipments to the ∞ \infty -categorical setting, termed ∞ \infty -equipments. These equipments take the form of particular double ∞ \infty -categories that support a notion of internal formal higher category theory. We will explore several examples of ∞ \infty -equipments. In particular, we will study the prototypical example given by the ∞ \infty -equipment of ∞ \infty -categories, and construct ∞ \infty -equipments of ∞ \infty -categories internal to general ∞ \infty -toposes as well. The final objective of this article is to study the fundamental notions of formal category theory within an arbitrary ∞ \infty -equipment. To this end, we not only follow the classical approach but also expand upon it.



1.
Introduction

In the field of
formal category theory
, the goal is to distill the fundamental concepts that enable one to make sense of category theory internal to a 2-category;
the archetype being the ambient 2-category of (small) categories. Ideally, one would then obtain an umbrella framework
that encompasses enriched category theory, indexed category theory, internal category theory, and fibered category theory.
In this paper, we will aim to extend the methods from this field to the higher categorical setting.

Partly, this is already being pursued by Riehl and Verity in their successful
synthetic
or
formal
treatise of the foundations
of
\infty
-category theory. Nowadays, one can find a complete introduction to
\infty
-category theory from this perspective
[
RV22
]
. The formal (higher) category theory
one will encounter in this paper, is, in contrast, not designed to study
\infty
-categories ex nihilo.
Instead, we will use the strength of existing
\infty
- and
(\infty,2)
-categorical methods to set up formal category theories internal
to an
(\infty,2)
-category.
This can then be specialized to enriched, internal, indexed, and fibered
\infty
-category theory, and combined flavors of these.

One of the motivations for pursuing this project lies in giving new (conceptual) foundations of the theory of indexed (or parametrized)
\infty
-categories, particularly, equivariant
\infty
-categories. Indexed

\infty
-category theory
was introduced by Barwick, Dotto, Glasman, Nardin and Shah in
[
BDG+16
]
. Its
fundamental notions were
further studied by Shah
[
Sha18
]
. Another far-developed approach to indexed
\infty
-category theory,
more generally, to internal
\infty
-category theory, is due
to Martini and Wolf
[
MW21
]
. In the sequel to this paper
[
Rui
]
, we will develop
machinery to formally retrieve the fundamental results of
[
Sha18
]
using the theory of this article.

The starting insight in the development of formal category theory is the following: merely having the structure of an ambient 2-category or more generally, an ambient
(\infty,2)
-category, is inadequate to define all desirable (internal) categorical notions.
Such a structure would give a good notion of (internal) adjunctions. However, the ambient
(\infty,2)
-category is incapable of
expressing the expected defining properties of fully faithful arrows or pointwise Kan extensions in general, to name just two examples.

Let us illustrate this with a first naive approach.
If
\mathscr{X}
is a
(\infty,2)
-category
1
1

1

We will make precise which model we use in
2
.
(or, for illustrative purposes, a 2-category), one could attempt to define a notion of fully faithful arrows as follows. Namely, say that an arrow
f:x\rightarrow y
in
\mathscr{X}

is fully faithful if and only if for every object
z\in\mathscr{X}
, the induced pushforward functor

f_{*}:\mathscr{X}(z,x)\rightarrow\mathscr{X}(z,y)

is fully faithful on mapping
\infty
-categories. This recovers the notion of fully faithful functors if
\mathscr{X}
is the
(\infty,2)
-category of (small)
\infty
-categories.
However, one can check that, in general, this definition does not recover the correct classical notion of enriched
fully faithful functors if
\mathscr{X}
is the ambient 2-category of enriched categories over an arbitrary monoidal category.

One could also attempt to define a notion of Kan extensions in
\mathscr{X}
. Namely, one could say that the datum of a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

in
\mathscr{X}
exhibits
g
as the (naive) left Kan extension of
f
if and only if the 2-cell witnesses the presheaf

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{X}(i,x)}(f,w^{*}(-)):\mathscr{X}(j,x)\rightarrow\mathscr{S}

to be representable by
g
. Here
\mathscr{S}
denotes the
\infty
-category of
spaces
or
\infty
-groupoids
. This definition already fails to capture the notion of pointwise left Kan extensions if

\mathscr{X}
is the ambient 2-category of categories. In this case, every pointwise left Kan extension is a naive left Kan extension,
but the converse is not necessarily true (see
[
Cam15
]
for some counterexamples). Moreover, this naive definition does not come with a method to compute such extensions.

These illustrative examples bear witness to the fact that having just an ambient 2-category or
(\infty,2)
-category,
does not suffice to define basic categorical notions. Consequently, one will need structures that are better suited for this purpose.
A first successful approach to formal category theory is due to the Australian school of category theorists. In
[
SW78
]
, Street and Walters axiomatize
so-called
Yoneda structures
. These are structures on 2-categories that allow one to do category theory in these 2-categories.
The name of these structures already reflects what obstructs the development of formal category theory in a plain ambient 2-category. Namely, a 2-category
does not come with natural notions of presheaves and representable presheaves. These notions are crucial to encode
desirable universal properties of pointwise Kan extensions.

Since the work of Street and Walters, there have been more approaches to formal category theory that saw the light of day. The famous approach of Wood
is of importance to us. In
[
Woo82
]
, Wood builds
on the ideas of Street and Walters and introduces
proarrow equipments
(sometimes, simply called
equipments
). We are interested in Verity’s equivalent
double categorical
perspective on these equipments
[
Ver92
]
(for the relation to Wood’s original formulation, see
[
Ver92
, Definition 1.2.4]
and
[
Shu08
, Appendix C]
).

To a great extent, this paper is concerned with generalizing the theory of proarrow equipments
to the
\infty
-categorical context. We make use of the formalism of
double
\infty
-categories
.
A double
\infty
-category is a 2-dimensional
\infty
-categorical construct that has (a space of) objects and
two types of (spaces of) arrows (1-cells) between objects:
vertical arrows
and
horizontal arrows
.
Moreover, a double
\infty
-category contains (a space of) 2-cells. A 2-cell may be pictured as a square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d,}

where the arrows pointing vertically (resp. horizontally) are vertical (resp. horizontal) arrows of the double
\infty
-category. Informally, a double
\infty
-category
may be viewed as the data of these spaces of objects, 1- and 2-cells, together with compatible and coherent composition laws for these cells. Double
\infty
-categories were first
considered by Haugseng
[
Hau13
]
and have also been studied by Moser in
[
Mos20
]
.

The
\infty
-categorical generalizations of proarrow equipments we will introduce, take the form of double
\infty
-categories which satisfy some additional properties.
The definition of these
\infty
-equipments
is more or less analogous to Verity’s formulation of classical proarrow equipments.
The horizontal arrows
of an
\infty
-equipment are called
proarrows
, which are used to encode desirable universal properties. Proarrows can be viewed as an abstraction of presheaves and they may be represented by vertical arrows.
The vertical arrows often play the role of generalized functors.
Imprecisely, an
\infty
-equipment is required to admit two specific ways in which each vertical arrow can represent a proarrow: either as a
companion
or
conjoint
.
Among other things, this formalism of proarrows and their representability ultimately allows one to express the desired universal property of pointwise Kan extensions in
6.3
.

Layout of this paper

Our first substantive section,
2
, contains the basic theory of (2-fold, 2-uple) Segal spaces, double
\infty
-categories,
(\infty,2)
-categories,
and introduces terminology that is used throughout the article.

In
3
, we will define
\infty
-equipments, the
\infty
-categorical analog of proarrow equipments.
Ultimately, we see that the classical definition may be (more or less) repeated in the higher context. This crucially builds on joint work in progress with Ozornova and Rovelli
[
ORR
]
where companions and conjoints in double
\infty
-categories are studied.
Because this is still unfinished, we will refer to the material in this future work as hypotheses.

The prototypical example of an
\infty
-equipment will be given by the equipment

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}

of
\infty
-categories (
4.2
). Its objects
are given by
\infty
-categories, the (vertical) arrows by usual functors, and the (horizontal) proarrows by
profunctors
. A profunctor
\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
between

\infty
-categories
is given by a functor
\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
.
Equivalently,
such a profunctor may be viewed as a
correspondence
or
two-sided discrete fibration
, similar to the classical case. As one might expect,
the construction
of this
\infty
-equipment is more involved than the construction of its classical counterpart. One possible construction using
Conduché fibrations
(or
exponentiable fibrations
) of this equipment
is implicit in the work of Ayala and Francis
[
AF20
]
. We will recall this construction in
4
. Moreover, we will
demonstrate that the underlying vertical
(\infty,2)
-category of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
is equivalent to a known model for the
(\infty,2)
-category of
\infty
-categories.

The construction of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
can be generalized. Let
\mathscr{E}
be an
\infty
-topos. Using closely related ideas of Haugseng
[
Hau17
]
, we will construct an
\infty
-equipment

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})

of
\infty
-categories internal to
\mathscr{E}
in
5
. We expect that its associated formal category theory recovers the internal
\infty
-category theory developed by Rasekh
[
Ras18
]
, and by Martini and Wolf
[
MW21
]
.

These formal category theories associated with
\infty
-equipments are studied in
6
, and it closely resembles the classical story. Most of the material that we will cover here, is inspired, or, is often completely analogous to the classical counterparts. Albeit in a different set-up,
some of the theory dates back to Street and Walters
[
SW78
]
, Guitart
[
Gui80
]
, and Wood
[
Woo82
]
. In the language
of classical double categorical equipments, parts of the formal category theory introduced here can be found in the work of Shulman
[
Shu13
]
.
Classical
virtual
equipments and their associated formal category play a role in the synthetic approach to
\infty
-category theory by Riehl and Verity as well
[
RV22
, Chapter 9]
. This work
has also been a source of inspiration.
In
6
, we will define notions
of
fully faithful arrows
,
weighted (co)limits
,
pointwise Kan extensions
and
exact squares
in an
\infty
-equipment, and study their properties.
Under an extra
pointedness
assumption on the equipments, one can also define notions of
conical (co)limits
, and
initial
and
final
arrows.
Under yet another extra (strong) assumption on the equipment,
one may even retrieve an internal form of Quillen’s theorem A, purely formally.

Preview of part II

We will further shape the theory of
\infty
-equipments in the sequel of this paper
[
Rui
]
. It is a general principle that one
may express more aspects of formal category theory when an
\infty
-equipment has more properties or is endowed with more structure. In the next article, we will consider

closed monoidal
\infty
-equipments
. This extra structure allows one to study the existence of internal arrows of Kan extension (i.e. adjoints to restriction arrows).
To this end, we will also need to set up a theory of
lax double functors
and
(lax) double adjunctions
. We will exhibit a supply of interesting examples. For instance, a geometric morphism between
\infty
-toposes
will induce a lax adjunction between their associated
\infty
-equipments of internal
\infty
-categories (constructed in
5
).
We study properties of (lax) double functors and adjunctions between
\infty
-equipments that allow one to relate the formal category theories associated with the corresponding
\infty
-equipments to each other.
The highlight of
[
Rui
]
is the study and construction of
cotensor
\infty
-equipments
. This construction allows one to construct new
\infty
-equipments out of existing ones by cotensoring with
\infty
-categories.
Applying this to the archetypical
\infty
-equipment
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
from
4
, one obtains
\infty
-equipments for indexed
\infty
-category theories that coincide
with the
\infty
-equipments for category theory internal to corresponding presheaf
\infty
-toposes of
5
. We study abstract properties of cotensor
\infty
-equipments that allow one to obtain the basic theory of indexed
\infty
-category theory established by Shah
[
Sha18
]
.
The expectation is that this approach is fruitful in the enriched setting as well, yielding similar results for indexed enriched
\infty
-category theory.

Food for thought

This article invites for further research. We list here some directions that would be worth pursuing:

(1)

We may apply the material in this article to the
\infty
-equipment of
\infty
-categories
enriched over a (suitably) cocomplete monoidal
\infty
-category.
A double
\infty
-category of enriched
\infty
-categories, functors and bimodules over a compatibly cocomplete monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathscr{V}
has already been constructed by Haugseng
[
Hau16
]
. One can show
that this is an
\infty
-equipment. In case that
\mathscr{V}
is given by the
\infty
-category
\mathscr{S}
of spaces, one should be able to show that this recovers
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
using
5.2
.
Now, interpreting the formal category theory of
6
inside the
\infty
-equipment of enriched
\infty
-categories,
one obtains the basic notions for enriched
\infty
-category theory. It would be worthwhile to flesh out the details. Moreover,
one could
compare the theory of weighted colimits that this yields to the one of Hinich
[
Hin21
]
.

(2)

One may try to compare the different models of enriched
\infty
-categories by not only setting up equivalences
between their ambient
\infty
-categories, but also setting up equivalences between their

\infty
-equipments.

(3)

It would be good to understand how far the formalization of category theory may be pushed. For instance, would
one be able to set up a formal theory of
doctrines
? Or may one formalize
Yoneda embeddings
?
Probably, more structure or properties are
needed of the
\infty
-equipments one deals with. An approach for the classical case can be found in
[
MT08
]
.

(4)

One should be able to repeat Shulman’s theory of
monoids in equipments

and
monoidal fibrations

[
Shu08
, Section 11, 12]
in the higher setting. How would one go about this?

(5)

The
\infty
-cosmology
developed by Riehl and Verity
[
RV22
]
is related to the theory of
\infty
-equipments. One
should be able to associate an
\infty
-equipment to an
\infty
-cosmos so that its ‘homotopy equipment’ recovers
the equipment studied in
[
RV22
, Chapter 9]
. One could try to make this precise.

2.
Two-dimensional
\infty
-category theory

We commence by recalling the theory of (
2-uple, 2-fold
)
Segal spaces
and
double
\infty
-categories
. Moreover, we will discuss Barwick’s model of
(\infty,2)
-categories via
complete
2-fold Segal spaces
[
Bar05
]
.

2.1.
Double Segal spaces

To efficiently introduce these concepts, we use the notion of
categorical objects
:

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be an
\infty
-category with finite limits.
Then a
categorical object
X
in
\mathscr{C}
is a simplicial object
X:\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}

such that the so-called
Segal map

X([n])\rightarrow X(\{0\leq 1\})\times_{X(\{1\})}\dotsb\times_{X(\{n-1\})}X(\{n-1\leq n\})

is an equivalence for all
n\geq 0
. The full subcategory of
\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{C})
spanned by the categorical objects in
\mathscr{C}
is denoted by
\textup{Cat}(\mathscr{C})
.

Definition 2.2
.

The following types of categorical objects have a special name:

(1)

A
Segal space
(resp.
Segal set
) is a categorical object in the
\infty
-category
\mathscr{S}
of spaces (resp. the category
\mathrm{Set}
of sets).
We write
\mathrm{Seg}(\mathscr{S}):=\textup{Cat}(\mathscr{S})
and
\mathrm{Seg}(\mathrm{Set}):=\textup{Cat}(\mathrm{Set})
for, respectively, the
\infty
-category
of Segal spaces and the category of Segal sets.

(2)

In turn, a
2-uple Segal space
or
double Segal space
is categorical object in
\textup{Cat}(\mathscr{S})
.
We will write

\textup{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S}):=\textup{Cat}(\textup{Cat}(\mathscr{S}))\subset\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op},\times 2},\mathscr{S})
for the
\infty
-category of 2-uple Segal spaces.

(3)

A
double
\infty
-category
is a categorical object in
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}
. We write
\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}:=\textup{Cat}(\textup{Cat}_{\infty})
for the

\infty
-category of double
\infty
-categories.

Unwinding the definition, we see that a 2-uple Segal space
\mathscr{P}
contains a space
\mathscr{P}_{0,0}
of objects (0-cells),
a space
\mathscr{P}_{0,1}
of
vertical arrows
(1-cells), a space
\mathscr{P}_{1,0}
of
horizontal arrows
(1-cells), and a space
\mathscr{P}_{1,1}
of
2-cells. The 2-cells and the compatible arrows of
\mathscr{P}
that have the same direction may be composed in a mutually compatible and coherently associative fashion. A 2-cell may be pictured as a square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d,}

where the arrows pointing vertically (resp. horizontally) are vertical (resp. horizontal) morphisms of
\mathscr{P}
. When
dealing with 2-cells in a 2-uple Segal space, we will usually leave out the notation
of the arrow (
\Rightarrow
) that fills the square.

We may view double
\infty
-categories as 2-uple Segal spaces with extra completeness conditions. Namely,
the famous result of Joyal and Tierney
[
JT07
]
asserts that the restriction of the Yoneda embedding

\textup{Cat}_{\infty}\rightarrow\textup{PSh}(\Delta):\mathscr{C}\mapsto([n]\mapsto\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}([n],\mathscr{C}))

is fully faithful, and its essential image consists of those Segal spaces which are
complete
:

Definition 2.3
.

A Segal space
X
is called
complete
if the degeneracy map

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Seg}(\mathscr{S})}([0],X)\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Seg}(\mathscr{S})}(J,X),

where
J
is the Segal set that sits in the pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]^{\sqcup 2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[3]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[0]^{\sqcup 2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0\leq 2\}\sqcup\{1\leq 3\}}

of Segal sets (this is a pushout square in
\mathrm{Seg}(\mathscr{S})
as well).

Remark 2.4
.

Viewed as a (discrete) Segal space,
J
is equivalent to the image of the free isomorphism
under the composite

\textup{Cat}\rightarrow\mathrm{Seg}(\mathrm{Set})\rightarrow\mathrm{Seg}(\mathscr{S}),

where the first arrow is the inclusion of categories inside Segal sets, and the second
arrow is the inclusion of discrete Segal spaces, i.e. it is induced by the inclusion
\mathrm{Set}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
. This fact was shown by Rezk
[
Rez01
, Proposition 11.1]
.

In light of this result of Joyal and Tierney, we obtain an induced fully faithful functor

\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}\rightarrow\textup{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S}):\mathscr{P}\mapsto(([n],[m])\mapsto\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}([m],\mathscr{P}_{n}))

whose essential image consists of all those 2-uple Segal spaces
X
so that
X_{n,\bullet}
is complete for all
n
.
This is readily checked to be an inclusion of a reflective subcategory.

Remark 2.5
.

The perspective of double Segal spaces turns out to be useful to prove certain results
of combinatorial nature, see for instance
[
Rui22
]
. In this article, we introduced a category of

2-dimensional pasting shapes
, denoted by
\mathrm{Shape}^{2}
.
These pasting shapes encode particular arrangements in which cells of 2-uple Segal spaces may be composed.
This is done using the language of
2-boxes
. A 2-box is simply a pair
(x,y)\in\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}\times\mathbb{N}^{\times 2}

of a top-left and bottom-right corner coordinate, so that
x_{a}\leq y_{a}
for all
a
. A 2-dimensional pasting shape is then simply
a collection of 2-boxes subject to some conditions (see
[
Rui22
, Definition 3.2]
for a precise definition). An illustrative
example can be found in
[
Rui22
, Example 3.4]
. The category
\mathrm{Shape}^{2}
comes, as expected, with a fully faithful nerve
functor

\mathrm{Shape}^{2}\rightarrow\textup{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S}),

which factors through
\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}
(Construction 3.10 of loc. cit.). The main result
[
Rui22
, Theorem 3.42]
stated that this nerve functor preserved particular kinds of colimits.

We will use the terminology of (2-dimensional) pasting shapes in a few instances throughout this article,
and, motivated by the fact that the nerve functor is fully faithful, we will make no distinction between
the notation of pasting shapes and their images under the nerve functor. Moreover, we will write

[a,b]\in\mathrm{Shape}^{2}

for the pasting shape that was previously denoted by
\square[a,b]
in
[
Rui22
]
. As a double Segal space, it is precisely the image of
([a],[b])
under the Yoneda embedding

\Delta^{\times 2}\rightarrow\textup{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})
.

In some proofs, we will also use the following notation. If
S,T
are subsets of
\mathbb{N}
, we will write

[S,T]\in\mathrm{Shape}^{2}

for the largest 2-dimensional pasting shape whose vertices are in
S\times T
, so that
[a,b]=[\{0,\dots,a\},\{0,\dotsc,b\}]
. Moreover,
if
\mathscr{P}
is a 2-uple Segal space and
I
is a pasting shape, then we write

\mathscr{P}(I):=\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})}(I,\mathscr{P}).

There are few involutive operations on
\textup{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})
:

Definition 2.6
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a 2-uple Segal space. Then the
horizontal opposite

\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{hop}}
is given by the composite

\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{hop}}:\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{(-)^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{id}}\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathscr{P}}\mathscr{S},

similarly, its
vertical opposite
is given by

\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{vop}}:\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\times(-)^{\mathrm{op}}}\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathscr{P}}\mathscr{S}.

Note that if
\mathscr{P}
is a double
\infty
-category,
then the vertical and horizontal opposites are again double
\infty
-categories.
Finally, we also have the
transpose

\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{t}}
of
\mathscr{P}
, given level-wise by

\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{t}}_{n,m}:=\mathscr{P}_{m,n}.

Example 2.7
.

In
4
, we will recall the construction of the double
\infty
-category

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}

of
\infty
-categories. Its objects are given by
\infty
-categories and the vertical arrows correspond to functors between
\infty
-categories. A horizontal arrow
\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}

between
\infty
-categories is given by a functor
\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
. This is also called a
profunctor
. A 2-cell in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

corresponds to a natural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{B}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\mathrm{op}}\times f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

The double
\infty
-category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
is a special example of a double
\infty
-category

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}),

which one can associate to any
\infty
-topos
\mathscr{E}
(see
5
), and whose
objects are given by
complete categorical objects in
\mathscr{E}
.

Example 2.8
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a monoidal
\infty
-category.
We further assume that

\mathscr{C}
admits all geometric realizations (i.e.
\mathscr{C}
has all colimits of
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
-shaped diagrams)
and that the tensor product
\otimes:\mathscr{C}^{\times 2}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
of
\mathscr{C}
preserves these geometric realizations in each variable.
Then Lurie constructs in
[
Lur17
, Remark 4.4.3.11]
an associated double
\infty
-category,

\mathbb{A}\mathrm{lg}(\mathscr{C})

called the
Morita double
\infty
-category
. Its objects are given by algebras in
\mathscr{C}
, the vertical arrows
are algebra morphisms, and a horizontal arrow
F:A\rightarrow B
between algebras corresponds to an
(A,B)
-bimodule
F
.
A 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

in this double
\infty
-category
corresponds to a map of
(A,B)
-bimodules
F\rightarrow A\otimes_{C}G\otimes_{D}B
.

Example 2.9
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be an
\infty
-category with finite limits. In
[
Hau18
]
, Haugseng constructs the double
\infty
-category

\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathscr{C})

of spans in
\mathscr{C}
. The objects of this double
\infty
-category are given by the objects of
\mathscr{C}
, and the vertical arrows are the arrows in
\mathscr{C}
.
A horizontal arrow
a\rightarrow b
in this double
\infty
-category is given by a span

a\leftarrow x\rightarrow b.

A 2-cell in
\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathscr{C})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

corresponds to a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

in
\mathscr{C}
.

2.2.
2-fold Segal spaces

Recall that 2-uple Segal spaces may be used as a model for
(\infty,2)
-categories. This a generalization
of Rezk’s model of complete Segal spaces for
\infty
-categories that is due to Barwick
[
Bar05
]
. Let us first
discuss the notion of
2-fold Segal spaces
and its relation to 2-uple Segal spaces:

Definition 2.10
.

A 2-uple Segal space
\mathscr{X}
is called a
2-fold Segal space
if the restriction
\mathscr{X}_{0,\bullet}:\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
is essentially constant.
We write
\mathrm{Seg}^{2}(\mathscr{S})
for the full subcategory of
\textup{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})
spanned by those 2-fold Segal spaces.

The canonical inclusion

(-)_{h}:\mathrm{Seg}^{2}(\mathscr{S})\rightarrow\textup{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})

of the full subcategory of 2-fold Segal spaces is called
the
horizontal inclusion
. By
[
Hau18
, Proposition 4.12]
, this admits a right adjoint, called the
horizontal fragment
functor

\mathrm{Hor}(-):\textup{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})\rightarrow\mathrm{Seg}^{2}(\mathscr{S}).

If
\mathscr{P}
is a 2-fold Segal space, then the horizontal fragment of
\mathscr{P}
is given level-wise by the pullback squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})_{n,m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{n,m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{0,0}^{\times(n+1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{0,m}^{\times(n+1)},}

see also
[
Hau18
, Remark 4.13]
.
Similarly, we have an adjunction between the
vertical inclusion
and
fragment
functor

(-)_{v}:\mathrm{Seg}^{2}(\mathscr{S})\rightleftarrows\textup{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S}):\mathrm{Vert}(-),

where

\mathscr{X}_{v}:=(\mathscr{X}_{h}^{\mathrm{t}})^{\mathrm{hop}},\quad\text{and},\quad\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P}):=\mathrm{Hor}((\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{hop}})^{\mathrm{t}}).

The fragment functors are compatible with opposites. We have the following special names for vertical and horizontal
opposites of 2-fold Segal spaces:

Definition 2.11
.

If
\mathscr{X}
is a 2-fold Segal space, then its
1-opposite
and
2-opposite

are defined by respectively
\mathscr{X}^{1-\mathrm{op}}:=\mathscr{X}^{\mathrm{hop}}
and
\mathscr{X}^{2-\mathrm{op}}
:=
\mathscr{X}^{\mathrm{vop}}
.

It readily follows that
\mathrm{Hor}(-)
and
\mathrm{Vert}(-)
are compatible with taking opposites as follows:
there are equivalences

\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{hop}})\simeq\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})^{1-\mathrm{op}}
,
\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{vop}})\simeq\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})^{2-\mathrm{op}}
,
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{hop}})\simeq\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{2-\mathrm{op}}

and
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{vop}})\simeq\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{1-\mathrm{op}}
that
are natural in
\mathscr{P}
.

Remark 2.12
.

With these observations in place, note that
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})\simeq\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{t}})^{2-\mathrm{op}}
and

\mathscr{X}_{v}\simeq(\mathscr{X}^{2-\mathrm{op}})_{h}^{\mathrm{t}}
.
One could also have defined the vertical inclusion and vertical fragments so that there do not appear 2-opposites in these descriptions.
However, the convention that we have introduced here, seems more natural regarding the
material that will follow.

To model
(\infty,2)
-categories via 2-fold Segal spaces, we need to invert the 2-categorical Dwyer-Kan equivalences in
\mathrm{Seg}^{2}(\mathscr{S})
.
This requires the introduction of a completeness condition:

Definition 2.13
.

A 2-fold Segal space
\mathscr{X}
is
said to be
complete
if it has the property that
\mathscr{X}_{1,\bullet}
and
\mathscr{X}_{\bullet,0}
are complete Segal spaces.

The full subcategory
\mathrm{Seg}^{2}(\mathscr{S})
spanned by the
complete 2-fold Segal spaces satisfies the axioms of
[
BSP21
]
for an
\infty
-category of
(\infty,2)
-categories.
Consequently, we will write

\textup{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}\subset\mathrm{Seg}^{2}(\mathscr{S})

for this full subcategory, and also refer to complete 2-fold Segal spaces as
(\infty,2)
-categories.
As expected, Moser showed
[
Mos20
, Theorem B]
that there is a fully faithful (or
nerve
) functor

\textup{Cat}_{2}\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{(\infty,2)},

where
\textup{Cat}_{2}
is the underlying
\infty
-category (actually, weak
(2,1)
-category) of the
canonical model structure on 2-categories

[
Lac02
]
. We will make
no distinction between 2-categories and the notation of images under this nerve functor. In practice, we will
just be interested in the restriction of the nerve to
gaunt 2-categories
, i.e. 2-categories without isomorphisms.
If
G
is a
gaunt 2-category
, i.e. a 2-category without isomorphisms, then, it is discrete as a complete 2-fold Segal space so that

G_{n,m}=\mathrm{Hom}([n;m,\dotsc,m],G),

the
set
of functors from the
globular 2-category

(ranging
n,m
, this naturally forms a double cosimplicial object)
to
G
.

A few comparisons of the model of complete 2-fold Segal spaces for
(\infty,2)
-categories and some other known models for
(\infty,2)
-categories
may be found in
[
Lur09b
]
,
[
BR13
]
and
[
BR20
]
.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
\mathscr{X}
be a 2-fold Segal space. Then the underlying Segal space
\mathscr{X}^{(1)}
of
\mathscr{X}
is given by
\mathscr{X}_{\bullet,0}
.
If
x
and
y
are objects of
\mathscr{X}
, then the
mapping Segal space

\mathscr{X}(x,y)
is defined by the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{X}(x,y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{X}_{1,\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{(x,y)\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{X}_{0,\bullet}^{\times 2}.}

A few of the important examples of 2-fold Segal spaces that we will encounter in this paper are not complete, but only locally in the following sense:

Definition 2.15
.

A 2-fold Segal space
\mathscr{X}
is called
locally complete
if the mapping Segal spaces
\mathscr{X}(x,y)
are complete for all
x,y\in\mathscr{X}
.

We will recall some results from
[
Hau18
]
:

Proposition 2.16
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{X}
is a 2-fold Segal space. Then
\mathscr{X}
is locally complete if and only if
\mathscr{X}_{n,\bullet}
is complete for all
n
.

Proof.

Note that complete Segal spaces are closed under limits. In light of the Segal conditions, we thus have to show that

\mathscr{X}
is locally complete if and only if
\mathscr{X}_{1,\bullet}
is complete. If
\mathscr{X}_{1,\bullet}
is complete, then the mapping Segal spaces of
\mathscr{X}
fit
in pullback squares of complete Segal spaces, hence they are again complete. The converse assertion follows from applying
[
Hau18
, Lemma 7.25]
to the source-target map

\mathscr{X}_{1,\bullet}\rightarrow\mathscr{X}_{0,\bullet}^{\times 2}
.
∎

Corollary 2.17
.

A 2-fold Segal space
\mathscr{X}
is complete if and only if it is locally complete and
\mathscr{X}^{(1)}
is complete.

Proof.

This follows from
[
Hau18
, Lemma 7.21]
.
∎

Definition 2.18
.

We say that a 2-uple Segal space is
(locally) horizontally complete
or
(locally) vertically complete

if
\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})
, respectively,
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
, is (locally) complete.

Throughout this article, vertically complete double
\infty
-categories will play an important role. We will write

\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}^{v}\subset\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}\subset\textup{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})

for the full subcategory of
\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}
spanned by the vertically complete double
\infty
-categories. Since the vertical fragment functor
\mathrm{Vert}(-)
is a right adjoint,
this is a reflective subcategory.

Remark 2.19
.

If
\mathscr{P}
is a double
\infty
-category, then
\mathscr{P}
is always locally horizontally complete. Namely, we have a pullback square of
Segal spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{(x,y)\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{0}^{\times 2},}

for
x,y\in\mathscr{P}
, where the right arrow is the source-target projection. However,
\mathscr{P}
is not necessarily
horizontally complete. Similarly, the vertical fragment of
\mathscr{P}
is not complete in general. If
x,y\in\mathscr{P}
, then the mapping Segal
space is given level-wise by

\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(x,y)_{n}\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{n}}(([n]\rightarrow[0])^{*}x,([n]\rightarrow[0])^{*}y).

Note that
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})^{(1)}\simeq\mathscr{P}_{0}
as Segal spaces, hence
\mathscr{P}
is vertically complete if and only if
\mathscr{P}
is locally vertically complete.

Finally, there is also a local notion for fully faithfulness:

Definition 2.20
.

A map
F:\mathscr{X}\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}
between 2-fold Segal spaces is called
locally fully faithful
if and only if the
induced map
\mathscr{X}(x,y)\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}(Fx,Fy)
is fully faithful for all
x,y\in\mathscr{X}
.

Using the language of pasting shapes (see
2.1
), we can give a geometric characterization of locally fully faithful maps.
In what follows, we will write
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq 1}I
for the
1-truncation
of a 2-dimensional pasting shape
I
as defined in
[
Rui22
, Definition 3.13]
.

Proposition 2.21
.

Let
F:\mathscr{X}\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}
be a map between 2-fold Segal spaces. Then the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

F
is locally fully faithful,

(2)

for any 2-dimensional locally composable pasting shape
I
, the induced map

\mathscr{X}(I)\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}(I)\times_{\mathscr{Y}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq 1}I)}\mathscr{X}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq 1}I)

is an equivalence.

Proof.

It follows from the pasting theorem of
[
Rui22
]
that (2) holds if and only if (2) holds for
I=[1,1]
.
The latter assertion is equivalent to the statement that the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{X}_{1,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Y}_{1,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{X}_{1,0}^{\times 2}\times_{\mathscr{X}_{0,0}^{\times 4}}{\mathscr{X}_{0,0}^{\times 2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Y}_{1,0}^{\times 2}\times_{\mathscr{Y}_{0,0}^{\times 4}}{\mathscr{Y}_{0,0}^{\times 2}}}

is a pullback square (here we used that
\mathscr{X}_{0,\bullet}
and
\mathscr{Y}_{0,\bullet}
are essentially constant). To this end,
it suffices to show that for all
x,y\in\mathscr{X}
the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{X}_{1,1}\times_{\mathscr{X}_{0,0}^{\times 4}}\{(x,y)\}^{\times 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Y}_{1,1}\times_{Y_{0,0}^{\times 4}}\{(Fx,Fy)\}^{\times 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{X}_{1,0}^{\times 2}\times_{\mathscr{X}_{0,0}^{\times 4}}\{(x,y)\}^{\times 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Y}_{1,0}^{\times 2}\times_{\mathscr{Y}_{0,0}^{\times 4}}\{(Fx,Fy)\}^{\times 2}}

is a pullback square.
But this may be identified with the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{X}(x,y)_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Y}(Fx,Fy)_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{X}(x,y)_{0}^{\times 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Y}(Fx,Fy)_{0}^{\times 2}}

which is a pullback square for all
x,y\in\mathscr{X}
if and only if
F
is locally fully faithful.
∎

2.3.
Adjunctions in 2-fold Segal spaces

Let us recall the notion of adjunctions in terms of pastings:

Definition 2.22
.

Let
\mathscr{X}
be a 2-fold Segal space. Then a pair
(f,g)
of arrows
f:x\rightarrow y
and
g:y\rightarrow x
in
\mathscr{X}
is called an
adjoint pair
,
if there exists two 2-cells, a unit and counit respectively:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

So that these 2-cells satisfy the triangle identities:

\hbox to168.4pt{\vbox to81.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 84.19893pt\lower-38.35419pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

In this case,
f
is the
left adjoint
and
g
is the
right adjoint
.

Proposition 2.23
.

Suppose that
F:\mathscr{X}\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}
is a locally full faithful map between 2-fold Segal spaces.
Let
f:x\rightarrow y
and
g:y\rightarrow x
be arrows in
\mathscr{X}
. Then
(f,g)
is an adjoint pair in
\mathscr{X}

if and only if
(Ff,Fg)
is an adjoint pair in
\mathscr{Y}
.

Proof.

Since
F
is locally faithful, we obtain (essentially unique)
liftings of the unit and counit of the adjunction
(Ff,Fg)
. These liftings will again satisfy the triangle identities.
This can readily be verified by applying characterization (2) of
2.2
to the (composable) pasting shapes
that govern the pastings of the triangle identities.
∎

Note that there are no completeness assumptions in the definition of the adjunctions. We will however be interested
in the case that the 2-fold Segal spaces are at least locally complete. In this case, we already have the following two properties:

Proposition 2.24
.

Suppose that

f:x\rightleftarrows y:g

is an adjunction in a locally complete 2-fold Segal space
\mathscr{X}
.
2
2

2

We adhere to the convention that the left adjoint arrow appears on top.
Then
any arrow
f^{\prime}:x\rightarrow y
is a left adjoint to
g
if and only if
f\simeq f^{\prime}
in
\mathscr{X}(x,y)
. Dually,
any arrow
g^{\prime}:y\rightarrow x
is a right adjoint to
f
if and only if
g\simeq g^{\prime}
in
\mathscr{X}(y,x)
.

Proof.

The proof goes precisely the same as in the classical 2-categorical case using pastings. To make this work, the local completeness of
\mathscr{X}
has
to come into play in the use of the fact that the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1,\{0,2\}]\sqcup[1,\{0,3\}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1,3]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1,0]}\sqcup{[1,0]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1,0]}}

is a pushout square of
locally complete
2-fold Segal spaces (this follows from
2.2
and
2.1
).
∎

Remark 2.25
.

Let
\mathfrak{adj}
be the
free adjunction
2-category (see
[
RV16
]
for a construction).
There are maps
l:[1]\rightarrow\mathfrak{adj}
and
r:[1]\rightarrow\mathfrak{adj}
that select, respectively, the left and right adjoint arrow.
Suppose that
\mathscr{X}
is an
(\infty,2)
-category.
Then Riehl and Verity demonstrate
[
RV16
, Theorem 4.3.9]
that if
(f,g)
is an adjunction in
\mathscr{X}
, then there exists a functor

a:\mathfrak{adj}\rightarrow\mathscr{X}

so that
al\simeq f
and
ar\simeq g
(and this extension is essentially unique
if one fixes either a unit or counit, see Theorem 4.4.11 of loc. cit.). Moreover, they prove in
[
RV16
, Theorem 4.4.18]
that the restriction maps

l^{*},r^{*}:\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}}(\mathfrak{adj},\mathscr{X})\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}}([1],\mathscr{X})

are equivalences onto the spaces spanned by, respectively, the left and right adjoint arrows in
\mathscr{X}
.
From this, the preceding two propositions may also be deduced (in the special case that
\mathscr{X}
is complete). Here, we implictly used
the equivalence between
\textup{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}
and the underlying
\infty
-category of the model category of (Joyal)
\mathrm{sSet}
-enriched categories (cf.
4.3
), constructed by Lurie
[
Lur09b
]
.

Proposition 2.26
.

Suppose that

f:x\rightleftarrows y:g

is an adjunction in a locally complete 2-fold Segal space
\mathscr{X}
. Then for every
z\in\mathscr{X}
,
we obtain induced adjunctions

f_{*}:\mathscr{X}(z,x)\rightleftarrows\mathscr{X}(z,y):g_{*}

g^{*}:\mathscr{X}(y,z)\rightleftarrows\mathscr{X}(x,z):f^{*}

of
\infty
-categories.

Proof.

Let
\eta
and
\epsilon
be respectively the unit and counit for the adjunction
(f,g)
. These are classified
by maps
[1]\rightarrow\mathscr{X}(x,x)
and
[1]\rightarrow\mathscr{X}(y,y)
respectively. They give rise to natural transformations

\eta_{*}:[1]\times\mathscr{X}(z,x)\rightarrow\mathscr{X}(x,x)\times\mathscr{X}(z,x)\rightarrow\mathscr{X}(z,x)

and

\epsilon_{*}:[1]\times\mathscr{X}(z,y)\rightarrow\mathscr{X}(y,y)\times\mathscr{X}(z,y)\rightarrow\mathscr{X}(z,y)

which are (resp.) our candidates for the unit and counit of the desired adjunction
(f_{*},g_{*})
. The triangle identities
for
\eta
and
\epsilon
now readily imply that the triangle identities hold for
(\eta_{*},\epsilon_{*})
. The other case is handled similarly.
∎

Definition 2.27
.

We say that a locally complete 2-fold Segal space
\mathscr{X}
is
closed
if for any arrow
f:x\rightarrow y
in
\mathscr{X}
,
the induced functors

f_{*}:\mathscr{X}(z,x)\rightarrow\mathscr{X}(z,y)

and

f^{*}:\mathscr{X}(y,z)\rightarrow\mathscr{X}(x,z)

admit right adjoints for every
z\in\mathscr{X}
.

A locally horizontally (resp. vertically) complete 2-uple Segal space
\mathscr{P}
is said to be
horizontally
(resp.
vertically
)
closed

if
\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})
(resp.
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
) is closed.

3.
Companions, conjoints and
\infty
-equipments

In this section, we will define
\infty
-equipments. Essentially, this section shows that the definition of classical equipments can be
repeated in the higher setting.

3.1.
Companions and conjoints

Like in the classical setting of proarrow equipments, the notion of
companions
and
conjoints
will be central to the theory of
\infty
-equipments.
These notions can be used to interpolate between the vertical and horizontal fragments of a double
\infty
-category.

Informally, the process of constructing an
\infty
-equipment with vertical fragment given by an
(\infty,2)
-category
\mathscr{X}
should
be viewed as
equipping
\mathscr{X}
with
proarrows
. As sketched in the introduction, these proarrows are essential to formulate an internal category theory for
\mathscr{X}
.
In the case that
\mathscr{X}
is the
(\infty,2)
-category of
\infty
-categories, the proarrows are given by
profunctors
(see
2.1
). Given a functor

f:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
, we can associate two canonical ‘representable’ profunctors to it. Namely, there is a
companion
profunctor
\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}

given by

\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}:(d,c)\mapsto\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{D}}(d,fc),

and a
conjoint
profunctor
\mathscr{D}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
given by

\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{D}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}:(c,d)\mapsto\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{D}}(fc,d).

This phenomenon is formalized in the following definition (cf.
[
GP04
, Subsection 1.1]
and
[
Shu08
, Definition A.1]
):

Definition 3.1
.

Let
f:x\rightarrow y
be a vertical arrow in a 2-uple Segal space
\mathscr{P}
. Then a
companion for
f

is a horizontal arrow
{y}_{f}:x\rightarrow y
in
\mathscr{P}
which admits two 2-cells in
\mathscr{P}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y}_{f}}

which paste together as follows:

Remark 3.2
.

A horizontal arrow
F
is a conjoint for a vertical arrow
f
if and only if
F
is a companion for
f^{\mathrm{op}}
in
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{vop}}
. Or similarly,

F
is a conjoint for
f
if and only if
F^{\mathrm{op}}
is a companion for
f
in
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{hop}}
.

Example 3.3
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be an
\infty
-category with finite limits and consider the associated double
\infty
-category of spans
\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathscr{C})
(see
2.1
).
A vertical arrow
f:x\rightarrow y
of
\mathbb{S}\mathrm{pan}(\mathscr{C})
is precisely an arrow in
\mathscr{C}
, and it admits both a companion and a conjoint. Namely, the companion

{y}_{f}
is given by the span

x=x\xrightarrow{f}y.

and the conjoint
{}_{f}{y}
is given by span

y\xleftarrow{f}x=x.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
f
be a vertical arrow in a double Segal space
\mathscr{P}
that admits both a conjoint and companion. Then the companion
{y}_{f}
is left adjoint to the conjoint
{}_{f}{y}
in
\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})
.

Proof.

It is proven in exactly the same manner as in the classical case
[
Shu08
, Proposition 5.3]
. For completeness, we repeat it here.
For the unit, we take the pasting
\eta

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{f}{y}}

For the counit, we take the pasting
\epsilon

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{f}{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

It is readily verified that this choice of unit and counit satisfies the triangle identity. We will demonstrate just one of them:

\hbox to168.85pt{\vbox to82.25pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 84.42577pt\lower-39.80275pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

In
[
ORR
]
, we will thoroughly study the notion of companions. We will briefly summarize the main prospected results of this work here, which will henceforth be labeled as hypotheses.
An important tool
in understanding companions which is central to loc. cit., is the
square
or
quintet functor
. In the classical setting, this construction goes back to Ehresmann
[
Ehr63
]
. In the (classical) context of
companions and conjoints, this functor has been considered by Grandis and Paré in
[
GP04
]
.

Definition 3.5

(
[
ORR
]
)
.

The
square functor

\textup{Sq}:\textup{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}\rightarrow\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}^{v}

is given by

\textup{Sq}(\mathscr{X})_{n,m}:=\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}}([n]\otimes[m],\mathscr{X}).

Remark 3.6
.

Here the tensor product

-\otimes-:\textup{Cat}_{2}^{\times 2}\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}

denotes the
oplax Gray tensor product
for strict 2-categories (see
[
Gra74
]
for the original definition,
and
[
Lac02
]
for compatibility with the canonical model structure), followed by the inclusion
\textup{Cat}_{2}\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}
. We will just make use of the double cosimplicial gaunt 2-category

\Delta\times\Delta\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}:([n],[m])\mapsto[n]\otimes[m].

In this paper, we will not be needing
an
(\infty,2)
-categorical (enhanced) version of the Gray tensor product, which is still an active subject of research (e.g. see the introduction of
[
CM23
]
for a summary of the current state of affairs). However, in the sequel of this paper

[
Rui
]
, we will make use of such an enhancement, and be careful about the properties we use.

In
[
ORR
]
, we aim to show the following:

Hypothesis 3.7

(
[
ORR
]
)
.

The square functor admits a right adjoint

\textup{Comp}:\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}^{v}\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}.

Construction 3.8
.

Note that there exists (canonical)
tautological double functors

[0,1]=[1]_{v}\rightarrow\textup{Sq}([1]),\quad\text{and},\quad[1,0]=[1]_{h}\rightarrow\textup{Sq}([1]),

which select the identity
[1]=[0]\otimes[1]\rightarrow[1]
and
[1]=[1]\otimes[0]\rightarrow[1]
,
respectively.

respectively.
The defined tautological maps are compatible with the face maps
[1]\rightrightarrows[1;1]
between the free 1- and 2-cell. Since
\textup{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}
is generated by these free cells,
and
(-)_{v}
,
(-)_{h}
and
\textup{Sq}(-)
preserve colimits, one can extend the above tautological maps to obtain
natural tautological maps

\mathscr{X}_{v}\rightarrow\textup{Sq}(\mathscr{X})\leftarrow\mathscr{X}_{h},

for any
(\infty,2)
-category
\mathscr{X}
.

Definition 3.9
.

The
free companion
is the double category given by

\mathfrak{comp}:=\textup{Sq}([1]).

Hypothesis 3.10

(
[
ORR
]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a vertically complete double
\infty
-category.
Then the tautological inclusion
[1]_{v}=[0,1]\rightarrow\mathfrak{comp}
induces an equivalence

\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}}(\mathfrak{comp},\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}}([0,1],\mathscr{P})=\mathscr{P}_{0,1}

onto the space of vertical arrows of
\mathscr{P}
that admit a companion. Similarly,
the tautological inclusion
[1]_{h}=[1,0]\rightarrow\mathfrak{comp}
gives rise to an equivalence

\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}}(\mathfrak{comp},\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}}([1,0],\mathscr{P})=\mathscr{P}_{1,0}

onto the space of horizontal arrows of
\mathscr{P}
that are a companion.

Remark 3.11
.

More generally, the theorem holds for any 2-uple Segal space
\mathscr{P}
which has the property that

\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})_{1,\bullet}
and
\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})_{1,\bullet}

are both complete.

Note that if
\mathscr{P}
is a vertically complete double
\infty
-category, then the
tautological maps of
3.1
induce (natural) projections

\textup{Comp}(\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
and
\textup{Comp}(\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})
.

Hypothesis 3.12

(
[
ORR
]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a vertically complete double
\infty
-category. Then
\mathscr{P}
admits all companions
if and only if the functor

\textup{Comp}(\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})

is an equivalence between
(\infty,2)
-categories.

Corollary 3.13
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is a vertically complete double
\infty
-category that admits all companions.
Then there exists a map, the companion embedding,

\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})

between locally complete 2-fold Segal spaces, which is locally fully faithful, acts as the identity on
objects, and carries vertical arrows to companions.

Proof.

It follows from
3.1
that the functor
\textup{Comp}(\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
admits
an inverse. Composing this inverse with the projection
\textup{Comp}(\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})
, we obtain the desired map.
∎

All these results admit the appropriate dual versions for conjoints, which we will also use in the rest of this paper.
These versions may be obtained from the previous results by introducing horizontal opposites.
For completeness, we record these here:

Definition 3.14
.

The
free conjoint
is given by

\mathfrak{conj}:=\mathfrak{comp}^{\mathrm{hop}}.

Corollary 3.15
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a vertically complete double
\infty
-category.
Then the tautological inclusion
[0,1]\rightarrow\mathfrak{conj}
induces an equivalence

\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}}(\mathfrak{conj},\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}}([0,1],\mathscr{P})=\mathscr{P}_{0,1}

onto the space of vertical arrows of
\mathscr{P}
that admit a conjoint. Similarly,
the tautological inclusion
[1,0]\rightarrow\mathfrak{conj}
gives rise to an equivalence

\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}}(\mathfrak{conj},\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}}([1,0],\mathscr{P})=\mathscr{P}_{1,0}

onto the space of horizontal arrows of
\mathscr{P}
that are a conjoint.

Corollary 3.16
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is a vertically complete double
\infty
-category that admits all conjoints.
Then there exists a map, the conjoint embedding,

\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})^{1,2-\mathrm{op}}

between locally complete 2-fold Segal spaces, which is locally fully faithful, acts as the identity on
objects, and carries vertical arrows to conjoints.

3.2.
Higher proarrow equipments

Our next goal is to introduce the notion of
\infty
-equipments. Classically, there are several equivalent ways
of defining these. In the higher setting, this is still true, albeit it is more complicated to demonstrate that the characterizations
agree. We will show this here. Let us start with a few preparatory definitions. Here, we use the language of pasting shapes of
[
Rui22
]

(see
2.1
for a brief and rough summmary).

Definition 3.17
.

The
niche

N\subset[1,1]

is the largest subshape of
[1,1]

that does not contain the 1-box
(0,0)\rightarrow(1,0)
. Pictorially,
N
is given by

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

A
niche
in a double Segal space
\mathscr{P}
is a map
n:N\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
. An extension
[1,1]\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
of such a niche is called
a
filler of
n
.

Throughout this subsection, we will consider the subshape

{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}\subset[1,2]

which is the largest subshape of
[1,2]
that does not contain the 1-box
(0,1)\rightarrow(1,1)
.
Note that there is a canonical inclusion

N\rightarrow{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}},

which is restricted from the inclusion
[1,1]\rightarrow[1,2]
that selects the bottom box
[1,\{1,2\}]
.

Proposition 3.18
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a double
\infty
-category and suppose that
n
is a niche in
\mathscr{P}
with filler
c
. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

the restriction map

\mathscr{P}([1,2])\times_{\mathscr{P}([1,1])}\{c\}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}({\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}})\times_{\mathscr{P}(N)}\{n\}

is an equivalence,

(2)

c
classifies a cartesian edge of the source-target projection
\mathscr{P}_{1}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{0}^{\times 2}
.

Proof.

Note that the map of item (1) lies over the space of horizontal arrows of
\mathscr{P}

via the tautological inclusion
[1,0]\rightarrow{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}\rightarrow[1,2]
that selects the horizontal 1-box
(0,0)\rightarrow(1,0)
,
so that the map fits in a diagram of spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L:=\mathscr{P}([1,2])\times_{\mathscr{P}([1,1])}\{c\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}({\vtop{\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}})\times_{\mathscr{P}(N)}\{n\}=:R,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}([1,0]).}

Hence, (1) holds if and only if the map

L_{F}:=\mathscr{P}([1,2])\times_{\mathscr{P}([1,\{0\}]\sqcup[1,\{1,2\}])}\{(F,c)\}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}({\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}})\times_{\mathscr{P}([1,\{0\}]\sqcup N)}\{(F,n)\}=:R_{F}

on fibers is an equivalence for every
F\in\mathscr{P}([1,0])
.
Since

[1,2]\simeq([1,\{1,2\}]\sqcup[\{0,1\},\{0\}])\cup_{[1,\{0\}]\sqcup[1,\{1\}]}[1,\{0,1\}],

we can identify the left hand side with

L_{F}\simeq(\mathscr{P}([1,\{0,1\}])\times_{\mathscr{P}([1,\{0\}]\sqcup[1,\{1\}])}\{(F,c|[1,\{0\}])\}\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{1}}(F,c|[1,\{0\}]).

We will similarly identify the fiber
R_{F}
. We consider the space
R^{\prime}_{F}
that sits in a diagram of pullback squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}({\vtop{\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\prime}_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}(\partial[1,2])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{(F,n\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}([1,0]\sqcup N).}

Note that we have a decomposition,

{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}\simeq\partial[1,2]\cup_{\partial[1,\{0,2\}]}[1,\{0,2\}],

so that we obtain a commutative diagram where all ticked squares are pullback squares,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}({\vtop{\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}([1,\{0,2\}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\prime}_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}(\partial[1,2])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}(\partial[1,\{0,2\}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{(F,n)\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}([1,0]\sqcup N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}([1,\{0\}]\sqcup[1,\{2\}]).}

From this, one deduces that
R_{F}
and
R^{\prime}_{F}
sit in a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}([1,\{0,2\}])\times_{\mathscr{P}([1,\{0\}]\sqcup[1,\{2\}])}\{(F,c|[1,\{1\}])\}=:S_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\prime}_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}(\partial[1,\{0,2\}])\times_{\mathscr{P}([1,\{0\}]\sqcup[1,\{2\}])}\{(c|[1,\{0\}],c|[1,\{1\}])\}=:S^{\prime}_{F},}

Hence, we deduce that

S_{F}\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{1}}(F,c|[1,\{1\}]).

Using the decompositions

\partial[1,2]\simeq([1,\{0\}]\sqcup N)\cup_{[\{0\},\{0\}]\sqcup[\{0\},\{1\}]\sqcup[\{1\},\{0\}]\sqcup[\{1\},\{1\}]}([\{0\},\{0,1\}]\sqcup[\{1\},\{0,1\}]),

\partial[1,\{0,2\}]\simeq([1,\{0\}]\sqcup[1,\{2\}])\cup_{[\{0\},\{0\}]\sqcup[\{0\},\{2\}]\sqcup[\{1\},\{0\}]\sqcup[\{1\},\{2\}]}([\{0\},\{0,2\}]\sqcup[\{1\},\{0,2\}]),

one sees that

R^{\prime}_{F}\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{0}}(F|[\{0\}],c|[\{0\},\{0\}])\times\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{0}}(F|[\{1\}],c|[\{1\},\{0\}]),

S^{\prime}_{F}\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{0}}(F|[\{0\}],c|[\{0\},\{1\}])\times\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{0}}(F|[\{1\}],c|[\{1\},\{1\}]).

Tracing through the identifications above, one sees that the map

L_{F}\rightarrow R_{F}

can be identified with the canonical comparison map that is an equivalence
for all
F\in\mathscr{P}([1,0])
if and only if
c
is cartesian arrow with respect to the functor
\mathscr{P}_{1}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{0}^{\times 2}.

∎

Definition 3.19
.

We call a 2-cell
c:[1,1]\rightarrow\mathscr{P}

cartesian
if the equivalent conditions of
3.2
are met.

There is also a dual notion of
cocartesian
fillers.

Definition 3.20
.

We define the
roof

R\subset[1,1]

to be the largest subshape of
[1,1]
which does not contain the 1-box

(0,1)\rightarrow(1,1)
.
A
roof
in a double Segal space
\mathscr{P}
is a map
R\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
. A
filler
for such a roof is an extension
to a map
[1,1]\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
.

A 2-cell
c:[1,1]\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
is called
cocartesian
if the 2-cell
c^{\mathrm{vop}}:[1,1]\simeq[1,1]^{\mathrm{vop}}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{vop}}

is cartesian in
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{vop}}
.

With the definitions, we then obtain directly from
3.2
:

Corollary 3.21
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a double
\infty
-category and suppose that
r
is a roof in
\mathscr{P}
with filler
c
. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

c
is a cocartesian filler for
r
,

(2)

the canonical map

\mathscr{P}([1,2])\times_{\mathscr{P}([1,1])}\{c\}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}({\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}})\times_{\mathscr{P}(R)}\{r\}

is an equivalence,

(3)

c
classifies a cocartesian edge of the source-target projection
\mathscr{P}_{1}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{0}^{\times 2}
.

The general theory of (co)cartesian fibrations immediately yields the following results:

Corollary 3.22
.

Suppose
\mathscr{P}
is a double
\infty
-category and that we have a diagram
p:[1,2]\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
in
\mathscr{P}
, that witnesses

e:=p|{[1,\{0,2\}]}
as the vertical composition of two 2-cells
c:=p|{[1,\{0,1\}]}
,
d:=p|{[1,\{1,2\}]}
.
Furthermore, assume that
c
is cartesian (resp. cocartesian). Then
d
is cartesian (resp. cocartesian) if and only if
e
is cartesian (resp. cocartesian).

Corollary 3.23
.

Any horizontal equivalence is (co)cartesian.

The notions of (co)cartesian fillers, companions and conjoints are closely related:

Proposition 3.24
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a vertically complete double
\infty
-category.
Suppose that we have a niche
n

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

in
\mathscr{P}
. Then if
f
has a companion and
g
has a conjoint, the pasting

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{g}{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

is a cartesian filler for
n
.

Dually, suppose that we have a roof
r

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

in
\mathscr{P}
. Then if
f
has a conjoint and
g
has a companion, the pasting

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{f}{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y}_{g}}

is a cocartesian filler for
r
.

We will give a proof of this later. The following choice of notation should now lead to no confusion:

Notation 3.25
.

In the first situation of
3.2
, we will denote the upper horizontal arrow that completes the niche
by

{}_{g}{F}_{f}:={}_{g}{y}\circ F\circ{x}_{f}.

In the second, dual case in
3.2
, we will write

{}^{g}{G}^{f}:={y}_{g}\circ G\circ{}_{f}{x}.

In particular, we have
{a}^{f}={}_{f}{x}
and
{}^{g}{b}={y}_{g}
so that equivalently,

{}^{g}{G}^{f}={}^{g}{b}\circ G\circ{a}^{f}.

Corollary 3.26
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is a vertically complete double
\infty
-category.
Let
f:x\rightarrow y
be a vertical arrow in
\mathscr{P}
. Then
\mathscr{P}
admits a cartesian filler of
the niches

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

if and only if
f
admits a companion, respectively, a conjoint.

Dually,
\mathscr{P}
admits a cocartesian filler of the roofs

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

if and only if
f
admits a companion, respectively, a conjoint.

Proof.

One direction follows from
3.2
.
The other direction can be deduced by factoring the identity 2-cell on
f
(suitably extended to

\sqcup

\sqcap

) via the universal properties
3.2
(1) and
3.2
(2) of (co)cartesian cells. To
check that the associated horizontal pastings are identities, one needs to use these universal properties once again.
∎

To prove
3.2
, we need the following observation:

Lemma 3.27
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is a double
\infty
-category, and
c:[1,1]\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
is a cell that factors through
a double
\infty
-category
C
such that the canonical map

C\cup_{N}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}\rightarrow C\cup_{[1,1]}[1,2]

is an equivalence. Then
c
is cartesian.

Proof.

By assumption,
c
factors as
[1,1]\rightarrow C\xrightarrow{c^{\prime}}\mathscr{P}
. This
gives rise to a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}}(C\cup_{[1,1]}[1,2],\mathscr{P})\times_{\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}}(C,\mathscr{P})}\{c^{\prime}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}([1,2])\times_{\mathscr{P}([1,1])}\{c\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}}(C\cup_{N}{\vtop{\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}},\mathscr{P})\times_{\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}}(C,\mathscr{P})}\{c^{\prime}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}({\vtop{\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}})\times_{\mathscr{P}(N)}\{c|N\},}

and by the pasting lemma for pullback squares, both horizontal arrows are equivalences. The left vertical arrow is an equivalence by assumption,
hence the right arrow must be an equivalence as well.
∎

Proof of
3.2
.

It suffices to show the first assertion since the dual assertion can be obtained by applying

(-)^{\mathrm{vop}}
.

Consider the thickened niche
N^{\prime}
defined by the following pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[0,1]}\sqcup{[0,1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{comp}\sqcup\mathfrak{conj}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{\prime}.}

Then the cell
c:[1,1]\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
we are interested in, factors
through the cell

[1,1]\rightarrow N^{\prime}

obtained by joining the appropriate cells
[1,1]\rightarrow\mathfrak{comp}\rightarrow N^{\prime}
,
[1,1]\rightarrow[1,0]\rightarrow N\rightarrow N^{\prime}
,

[1,1]\rightarrow\mathfrak{conj}\rightarrow N^{\prime}
.
In view of
3.2
, it suffices to show that the inclusion

i:{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}^{\prime}:=N^{\prime}\cup_{N}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}\rightarrow N^{\prime}\cup_{[1,1]}[1,2]=:[1,2]^{\prime}

is an equivalence of double
\infty
-categories.

To this end, we will exhibit an explicit inverse to
i
. We will make use of the following 2-dimensional pasting shape:

P:=\hbox to235.89pt{\vbox to123.28pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 117.94438pt\lower-61.63898pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {

{{{{{}}{

{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 70.69 -73.31 L 102.79 -73.31}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{74.48886pt}{-52.97925pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

There is a map
p:P\rightarrow{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}^{\prime}
represented by the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,2).}

Note that
P
admits an inclusion

[1,2]\rightarrow P
that factors the inclusion
[1,1]\rightarrow P
of the outer cell.
Restricting
p
along this inclusion, we obtain a double functor

q:[1,2]\rightarrow{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}^{\prime},

with the following properties:

•

the restriction
q|{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}
is equivalent to the canonical inclusion
{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}\rightarrow{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}^{\prime}
,

•

the composite
iq
is equivalent to the canonical inclusion
[1,2]\rightarrow[1,2]^{\prime}
.

These properties may be readily verified by the reader, using the companion and conjunction identities (cf. the proof of
[
Shu08
, Theorem 4.1]
).
Consequently, the map
q
gives rise to a double functor

r:[1,2]^{\prime}\rightarrow{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}^{\prime}

which is an inverse to
i
.
∎

We may combine the preceding few established results, to obtain the following consequence:

Corollary 3.28
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a vertically complete double
\infty
-category. Then the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

every vertical arrow in
\mathscr{P}
admits both a conjoint and a companion,

(2)

every niche in
\mathscr{P}
admits a cartesian filler,

(3)

every roof in
\mathscr{P}
admits a cocartesian filler,

(4)

the source-target projection
\mathscr{P}_{1}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{0}^{\times 2}
is a cartesian fibration,

(5)

the source-target projection
\mathscr{P}_{1}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{0}^{\times 2}
is a cocartesian fibration.

Definition 3.29
.

A double
\infty
-category
\mathscr{P}
is called an
\infty
-equipment
if it is vertically complete and the
equivalent conditions of
3.2
are met.
We write

\textup{Equip}_{\infty}\subset\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}

for the full subcategory spanned by the
\infty
-equipments.

Proposition 3.30
.

A double functor between
\infty
-equipments preserves (co)cartesian cells.

Proof.

This follows directly from the fact
that double functors preserve companions and conjoints and the description of (co)cartesian cells of
3.2
.
∎

Proposition 3.31
.

The subcategory
\textup{Equip}_{\infty}\subset\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}
is a reflective subcategory. In particular,

\infty
-equipments are closed under limits.

Proof.

Since
\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}^{v}
is a reflective subcategory of
\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}
, it suffices to show that

\textup{Equip}_{\infty}
is a reflective subcategory of
\textup{DblCat}_{\infty}^{v}
. This follows from
3.1

which asserts that

\infty
-equipments are those vertically complete double
\infty
-categories which are local with respect to the inclusions

[0,1]\rightarrow\mathfrak{comp}
and
[0,1]\rightarrow\mathfrak{conj}
.
∎

Example 3.32
.

The double
\infty
-categories of spans of
2.1
and
3.1
are examples of
\infty
-equipments.
Although we do not prove this here,
one can also show that the Morita double
\infty
-categories of
2.1
are
\infty
-equipments.

Convention 3.33
.

Throughout this article, we will adhere to the following terminology. If
\mathscr{P}
is an
\infty
-equipment, then vertical
arrows will plainly be called
arrows
, and its horizontal arrows will be called
proarrows
.

Remark 3.34
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is an
\infty
-equipment, and suppose that we have arrows
f:a\rightarrow c
,
g:b\rightarrow d

and proarrows
F:a\rightarrow b
,
G:c\rightarrow d
in
\mathscr{P}
. Then it can be deduced from the universal property of (co)cartesian cells,
or from combining
2.3
and
3.1
, that we have equivalences

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(c,d)}({}^{g}{F}^{f},G)\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(a,b)}(F,{}_{g}{G}_{f})

which are natural in
F
and
G
. We will use this interchanging property often when carrying out computations with proarrows.

4.
Example: the
\infty
-equipment of
\infty
-categories

Let us now consider the prototypical example of an
\infty
-equipment.
Our goal of this section is to recall and closely follow the construction given by Ayala and Francis in
[
AF20
]
, of a double
\infty
-category

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}

whose objects are (small)
\infty
-categories, whose vertical arrows are given by functors and whose horizontal arrows
given by
profunctors
, or, from an equivalent perspective, given by
two-sided discrete fibrations
(these are called

bifibrations
by Lurie in
[
Lur09a
]
) or
correspondences
.

4.1.
Correspondences, two-sided discrete fibrations and profunctors

As preparation, we will give a quick recollection of these (equivalent) notions.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
be two (small)
\infty
-categories. Then:

•

a
correspondence

F:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
is a functor

E\rightarrow[1]

together with equivalences
E_{1}\simeq\mathscr{C}
and
E_{0}\simeq\mathscr{D}
.

•

a
two-sided discrete fibration

F:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
is a span

\mathscr{C}\leftarrow E\rightarrow\mathscr{D}

so that the left leg is a cocartesian fibration, and the right leg is cartesian fibration, and moreover,
for any
d\in\mathscr{D}
, the pullback
E_{d}=E\times_{\mathscr{D}}\{d\}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
is a left fibration,
and for any
c\in\mathscr{C}
, the pullback
E_{c}=\{c\}\times_{\mathscr{C}}E\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
is a right fibration,

•

a
profunctor

F:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
is a functor

\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}.

These objects fit in ambient
\infty
-categories as follows.
The
\infty
-category of correspondences is given by
\mathrm{Corr}:=\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/[1]
, and the
\infty
-category of two-sided discrete fibrations is given by the full subcategory

\mathrm{DFib}\subset\mathrm{Fun}([1]\cup_{\{0\}}[1],\textup{Cat}_{\infty})

spanned by those spans that are two-sided discrete fibrations. Note that both
these
\infty
-categories are canonically fibered over
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}^{\times 2}
. The
\infty
-category of profunctors is obtained by unstraightening
the functor

\textup{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{op},\times 2}\rightarrow\widehat{\textup{Cat}}_{\infty}:(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})\mapsto\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{C},\mathscr{S})

to a (large) cartesian fibration

\mathrm{Prof}\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{\infty}^{\times 2}.

If
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
are
\infty
-categories, we will write
\mathrm{Corr}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
,
\mathrm{DFib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
and
\mathrm{Prof}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
for the fibers
above
(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
of the similary named
\infty
-categories, so that they are the ambient
\infty
-categories for correspondences, two-sided discrete fibrations and profunctors
from
\mathscr{C}
to
\mathscr{D}
, respectively.

Like in the classical case, profunctors, two-sided discrete fibrations and correspondences encode the same data. Precisely, in
[
AF20
, Subsection 4.1]
,
the following is shown:

Proposition 4.2

(
[
AF20
]
)
.

There are equivalences

\mathrm{DFib}\rightarrow\mathrm{Corr}\rightarrow\mathrm{Prof}

of
\infty
-categories fibered over
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}^{\times 2}
so that a two-sided discrete fibration
\mathscr{C}\leftarrow E\rightarrow\mathscr{D}

is carried
to the correspondence

\mathscr{C}\cup_{E\times\{1\}}E\times[1]\cup_{E\times\{0\}}\mathscr{D}\rightarrow[1],

and a correspondence
E\rightarrow[1]
is in turn carried to the profunctor

E_{0}^{\mathrm{op}}\times E_{1}\rightarrow E^{\mathrm{op}}\times E\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Map}_{E}}\mathscr{S}.

Remark 4.3
.

Another proof of the fiberwise equivalence between
\mathrm{DFib}
and
\mathrm{Corr}

(in the language of model categories) was first given by Stevenson in
[
Ste18
]
.

4.2.
Construction via Conduché fibrations

Central to the construction of the double
\infty
-category of
\infty
-categories by Ayala and Francis, is the notion of

Conduché fibrations
, also known as
exponentiable fibrations
.
These types of fibrations are direct generalizations of the
similarly named 1-categorical notions.

Definition 4.4
.

A functor
p:E\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
between
\infty
-categories is called a
Conduché fibration
