if the pullback functor

p^{*}:\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/E

preserves all colimits or, equivalently, is a left adjoint. The full subcategory of
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/\mathscr{C}
spanned by
the Conduché fibrations is denoted by
\mathrm{Con}(\mathscr{C})
.

Remark 4.5
.

In the model of quasi-categories, these fibrations are also known as
flat inner fibrations
and they were studied by Lurie in
[
Lur17
, Appendix B.3]
.

In the double
\infty
-category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
that we aim to construct, there is a natural candidate for the horizontal composition
of two correspondences
E\rightarrow[1]
and
E^{\prime}\rightarrow[1]
so that
E_{1}^{\prime}\simeq E_{0}\simeq\mathscr{D}
. Namely,
we could form the pushout
E\cup_{\mathscr{D}}E^{\prime}
, which is canonically fibered over
[2]
. Pulling back this pushout
along the inner face map
d_{1}:[1]\rightarrow[2]
, we obtain a candidate
E^{\prime}\circ E
for the composite of
E
and
E^{\prime}
. The following
characterization of Conduché fibrations asserts that Conduché fibrations
over
[2]
are precisely those functors that are obtained
by combining two correspondences via a pushout in the manner above:

Proposition 4.6

(
[
AFR18
, Lemma 5.16(3)]
)
.

A functor
p:E\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
is a Conduché fibration if and only if for every map
[2]\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
,
the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\times_{\mathscr{C}}\{1\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\times_{\mathscr{C}}\{0\leq 1\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\times_{\mathscr{C}}\{1\leq 2\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\times_{\mathscr{C}}[2]}

is a pushout square of
\infty
-categories.

Corollary 4.7
.

Conduché fibrations are closed under base change.

Moreover, using characterization (6) of
[
AFR18
, Lemma 5.16]
,
Ayala and Francis immediately deduce the following, which we state without proof (cf.
[
Lur17
, Example B.3.10]
):

Proposition 4.8

(
[
AF20
, Corollary 1.13]
)
.

Every correspondence is a Conduché fibration.

Construction 4.9
.

Since Conduché fibrations are closed under base change, the functor

\textup{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\widehat{\textup{Cat}}_{\infty}:\mathscr{C}\mapsto\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/\mathscr{C}

obtained by straightening the (large) cartesian fibration
\mathrm{ev}_{1}:\mathrm{Fun}([1],\textup{Cat}_{\infty})\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{\infty}
,
gives rise to a functor

\textup{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\widehat{\textup{Cat}}_{\infty}:\mathscr{C}\mapsto\mathrm{Con}(\mathscr{C}).

Restricting this functor along the

twisted inclusion

\mathrm{tw}:\Delta\xrightarrow{(-)^{\mathrm{op}}}\Delta\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{\infty},

where the last functor is given by the usual inclusion, we obtain a simplicial
\infty
-category

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}.

Ayala and Francis establish the following in the proof of
[
AF20
, Corollary 1.25]
:

Proposition 4.10

(
[
AF20
]
)
.

The simplicial
\infty
-category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
is a (large) double
\infty
-category.

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
be
\infty
-categories. Then there is an equivalence

\mathrm{Vert}(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty})(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})\simeq\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})

between
\infty
-categories.
In particular,
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
is vertically complete.

Proof.

In light of the discussion in
2.2
, we obtain natural identifications

\displaystyle\mathrm{Vert}(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty})(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})_{n}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Con}([n])}([n]\times\mathscr{C},[n]\times\mathscr{D})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}([n]\times\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}([n],\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})),

so that
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty})(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
is given by the complete Segal space associated
to
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
. Here, the contribution of the twist
(-)^{\mathrm{op}}
in the definition of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
is cancelled
by our definition of the vertical fragment (see
2.2
). The latter assertion follows from the same remark as well.
∎

Definition 4.12
.

We define the
(\infty,2)
-category of (small)
\infty
-categories to be

\textup{CAT}_{\infty}:=\mathrm{Vert}(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}).

Remark 4.13
.

This will be our preferred model. Shortly, in
4.3
,
we will compare this definition to Lurie’s model of the
(\infty,2)
-category of
\infty
-categories.

We want to demonstrate that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
is an
\infty
-equipment.
Since we have shown that it is vertically complete, it remains to verify that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
admits all companions and conjoints.

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
f:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
be a functor between
\infty
-categories.
As a vertical arrow in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
,
f

admits both a companion and conjoint which may be described as follows:

(1)

as correspondences, they are given by respectively the cartesian fibration that classifies

[1]^{\mathrm{op}}\simeq[1]\xrightarrow{f}\textup{Cat}_{\infty},

and the cocartesian fibration that classifies

f:[1]\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{\infty}
,

(2)

as two-sided discrete fibrations, they are given by respectively the spans

\mathscr{C}\leftarrow\mathscr{C}\times_{\mathrm{Fun}(\{1\},\mathscr{D})}\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathscr{D})\rightarrow\mathscr{D}

and

\mathscr{D}\leftarrow\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathscr{D})\times_{\mathrm{Fun}(\{0\},\mathscr{D})}\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C},

(3)

as profunctors, they are given by respectively

\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}:(d,c)\mapsto\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{D}}(d,fc)

and

\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{D}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}:(c,d)\mapsto\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{D}}(fc,d).

Consequently,
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
is an
\infty
-equipment.

Proof.

Let
f:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
be a functor between
\infty
-categories. Then we can consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

of
\infty
-categories. This diagram corresponds to a map of cocartesian fibrations

g:E\rightarrow E^{\prime}

above
[2]
under the straightening equivalence. We claim that
F:=E|\{1\leq 2\}\simeq E^{\prime}|\{0\leq 1\}
is the conjoint of
f
in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
witnessed
by the 2-cells
g|\{1\leq 2\}
and
g|\{0\leq 1\}
.
Note that
E
and
E^{\prime}
are in particular Conduché fibrations (see
[
AF20
, Lemma 2.15]
for a proof) so that the pullback of
d_{1}^{*}g
of
g
along the inner face map
d_{1}:[1]\rightarrow[2]

corresponds to the horizontal pasting

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

of
g|\{1\leq 2\}
followed by
g|\{0\leq 1\}
in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
. Via the straightening equivalence (and its naturality), we see that this pullback
d_{1}^{*}g
corresponds to the identity 2-cell on
F
.
The vertical pasting

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

corresponds to the composition
g|\{1\leq 2\}\circ g|\{0\leq 1\}
in
\mathrm{Corr}
. Using the straightening equivalence again, one sees that this
composition is equivalent to the identity 2-cell on
f
. This shows that
F
is the conjoint of
f
, as desired. A similar argument applies for the case of companions.
It follows that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
is an
\infty
-equipment.

Finally, we show (2) and (3). We can describe the horizontal identity arrow on an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
as follows. As a correspondence,
it is given by the projection
\mathscr{C}\times[1]\rightarrow[1]
. Using the identifications of
4.1
, one verifies that it given as a profunctor
and two-sided discrete fibration by
the mapping space functor
\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{C}}(-,-):\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
and the projection
(\mathrm{ev}_{1},\mathrm{ev}_{0}):\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\times 2}
respectively (cf.
[
AF20
, Example 4.3]
).
Now, we recall that
\mathrm{Prof}\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{\infty}^{\times 2}
classifies the functor
(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})\mapsto\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{C},\mathscr{S})
.
Thus we may deduce (3) from the description of its cartesian lifts of mapping space functors. In a similar fashion, (2) can be deduced. Recall that we have a fibered fully faithful
inclusion

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{DFib}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}([1]\cup_{\{0\}}[1],\textup{Cat}_{\infty}).}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Cat}_{\infty}^{\times 2}}

One can check that the right projection is a cartesian fibration. Cartesian lifts are created by constructing certain pullbacks, and
\mathrm{DFib}
contains all these relevant cartesian lifts. Now, (2) can be deduced from this description
of cartesian lifts.
∎

Identifying correspondences
with profunctors and two-sided discrete fibrations, the horizontal composition in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
can be described as follows:

Proposition 4.15

(
[
AF20
, Lemma 4.5]
)
.

Let
F:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
and
G:\mathscr{D}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
be proarrows of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
.
Then the composite
G\circ F
can be represented as follows:

(1)

If
F
and
G
are two-sided discrete fibrations given by spans
\mathscr{C}\leftarrow E\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
and
\mathscr{D}\leftarrow E^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
,
then
G\circ F
is the two-sided discrete fibration

\mathscr{C}\leftarrow(E\times_{\mathscr{D}}E^{\prime})[W^{-1}]\rightarrow\mathscr{E},

whose legs are induced by projection, and
where
W
is the subcategory spanned by the morphisms that are carried
to equivalences under the projection
E\times_{\mathscr{D}}E^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{E}
.

(2)

If
F
and
G
are profunctors, then the composite profunctor
G\circ F
is given by the coend formula

\mathscr{E}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}:(e,c)\mapsto\intop\nolimits^{d\in\mathscr{D}}G(e,d)\times F(d,c).

Using some (co)end calculus and the coend formula for the composite of profunctors, we may obtain the following result:

Corollary 4.16
.

The double
\infty
-category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
is horizontally closed. Suppose that
\mathscr{E}
is an
\infty
-category, and let
W:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
be a proarrow in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
. From the perspective of profunctors,
we have an adjunction

W_{*}:\mathrm{Prof}(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{C})\rightleftarrows\mathrm{Prof}(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{D}):{}^{W}{(-)},

where the right adjoint carries
F\in\mathrm{Prof}(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{D})
to the profunctor defined by the end

{}^{W}{F}(c,e)=\intop\nolimits_{d\in\mathscr{D}}\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{S}}(W(d,c),F(d,e)).

Similarly, we have an adjunction

W^{*}:\mathrm{Prof}(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{E})\rightleftarrows\mathrm{Prof}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{E}):(-)^{W},

where the right adjoint is given by

F^{W}(e,d)=\intop\nolimits_{c\in\mathscr{C}}\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{S}}(W(d,c),F(e,c))

for
F\in\mathrm{Prof}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{E})
.

Proof.

This result can readily be obtained from
4.2
(2) by
using Fubini’s theorem for ends
[
Lor18
]
(an alternative proof can be found in
[
Hau22
]
), and the fact that mapping spaces in functor categories can be computed
via ends (see for instance Section 5 of
[
GHN17
]
).
∎

Remark 4.17
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{E}
be
\infty
-categories. Taking
\mathscr{D}=[0]
,
4.2
has also
been proven using the perspective of two-sided discrete fibrations by Stevenson in
[
Ste18
, Proposition 4.24]
.
Suppose that
W:X\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
is a right fibration, i.e. a two-sided discrete fibration from
[0]
to
\mathscr{E}
. If
F:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
is a two-sided discrete fibration given by the span

\mathscr{C}\leftarrow E\rightarrow\mathscr{E},

then Stevenson proves that the two-sided discrete fibration

{}^{W}{F}:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow[0]
is the left fibration that sits in the following pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(X,E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(X,\mathscr{C})\times\mathrm{Fun}(X,\mathscr{E}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{W}{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(X,F)}

of
\infty
-categories, where the bottom arrow is the product of the diagonal and the constant functor that selects
W
.

Riehl and Verity have also given a proof of
4.2
, now in full generality, from the perspective of
two-sided discrete fibrations
[
RV22
, Theorem 12.3.6]
.

Remark 4.18
.

Passing to larger universes, one can repeat the same constructions in this section to obtain larger
\infty
-equipments for larger
\infty
-categories.
For instance, we have a (very large)
\infty
-equipment

\widehat{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}}_{\infty}

of large
\infty
-categories. The proarrows of this
\infty
-equipment
are again given by correspondences, two-sided discrete fibrations, and profunctors (but the latter are now valued in
\hat{\mathscr{S}}
, the
\infty
-category of large spaces).
Note that there is a canonical double functor
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}}_{\infty}
that is levelwise fully faithful.
Consequently, throughout this article, we will mostly be implicit about enlarging universes,
and hence leave out the decorations with hat symbols.

4.3.
Comparison of the vertical fragment

As stated in
4.2
, the vertical fragment
\textup{CAT}_{\infty}
of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
will be our preferred model of the
(\infty,2)
-category of

\infty
-categories. We will show that this is justified by comparing this vertical fragment
to another model for the
(\infty,2)
-category of
\infty
-categories due to Lurie, constructed in
[
Lur09b
]
.

Lurie has defined a model structure on the category of
marked simplicial sets

\mathrm{sSet}^{+},

which is equivalent to the Joyal model structure on simplicial sets (
[
Lur09a
, Chapter 3]
). We may then consider the category

\textup{Cat}^{\mathrm{sSet}^{+}}

of
marked simplicial categories
: categories enriched over
\mathrm{sSet}^{+}
(with respect to the cartesian monoidal structure).
This carries an induced model structure
[
Lur09a
, Section A.3]
. In
[
Lur09b
]
, Lurie (essentially) shows
that there exists a
nerve functor

N:\textup{Cat}^{\mathrm{sSet}^{+}}\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{(\infty,2)},

which carries the weak equivalences
W
between fibrants
to equivalences (by first passing through the category of
scaled simplicial sets
). He demonstrates that the induced derived functor

\textup{Cat}^{\mathrm{sSet}^{+},\circ}[W^{-1}]\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}

is an equivalence (cf. the discussion in
[
HHLN21
, Subsection 5.1]
). Here,
we wrote
\textup{Cat}^{\mathrm{sSet}^{+},\circ}\subset\textup{Cat}^{\mathrm{sSet}^{+}}
for the full subcategory spanned by the fibrant marked simplicial categories.
Similarly, we will write
\mathrm{sSet}^{+,\circ}\subset\mathrm{sSet}^{+}
for the full (marked simplicial) subcategory of
\mathrm{sSet}^{+}
spanned by the fibrants (i.e. quasi-categories with their equivalences marked).
Note that this yields a fibrant marked simplicial category.
The goal is now to show the following:

Proposition 4.19
.

There exists an equivalence of
(\infty,2)
-categories

\textup{CAT}_{\infty}\simeq N(\mathrm{sSet}^{+,\circ}).

To prove this, we will need a generalization of Lurie’s straightening theorem. Recall that this theorem sets up an equivalence

\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\textup{Cat}_{\infty})\rightarrow\mathrm{Cocart}(\mathscr{C}),

given by the unstraightening, for an
\infty
-category

\mathscr{C}
. Here

\mathrm{Cocart}(\mathscr{C})\subset\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/\mathscr{C}

is the subcategory spanned by the cocartesian fibrations and morphisms that preserve cocartesian edges. On account of
[
GHN17
, Proposition A.31]
,
the unstraightening functor is (coherently) natural in
\mathscr{C}
.

Following
[
HHLN21
]
, let us write

\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{C})\subset\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/\mathscr{C}

for the
full
subcategory spanned by the cocartesian fibrations.
Using Lurie’s
(\infty,2)
-categorical straightening theorem
[
Lur09b
, Theorem 3.8.1]
, Haugseng, Hebestreit, Linskens and Nuiten show a lax variant
of the
(\infty,1)
-categorical straightening theorem
[
HHLN21
, Theorem 5.3.1]
. This takes the form of an equivalence on level of
(\infty,2)
-categories between

(\infty,2)
-categories of functors and lax natural transformations, and
(\infty,2)
-categorical enhancements of the lax variants of the
\infty
-category of cocartesian fibrations.
In this article, we will not need the full strength of this equivalence, but a less general, specialized form of the lax straightening theorem:

Theorem 4.20

(
[
HHLN21
, Theorem 5.3.1]
)
.

There is an equivalence

\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}}([n]\otimes[m],N(\mathrm{sSet}^{+,\circ}))\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}([n],\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{lax}}([m])).

natural in
[n],[m]\in\Delta
.

Remark 4.21
.

In
[
HHLN21
]
, the authors use an
(\infty,2)
-categorical oplax Gray tensor product. Tensoring the 1-categories
[n]
and
[m]

via this refinement, they show in
[
HHLN21
, Proposition 5.1.9]
that the resulting
(\infty,2)
-category coincides with the classical Gray tensor product
[n]\otimes[m]
(see
3.1
).

There is one more ingredient needed to prove the comparison. This is a well-known fact,
and we refer to
[
HHLN21
, Lemma 5.1.11]
for a proof:

Lemma 4.22
.

Let
n,m\geq 0
.
Then there is a pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprodop\displaylimits_{0\leq i\leq n}\{i\}\otimes[m]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]\otimes[m]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprodop\displaylimits_{0\leq i\leq n}\{i\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n;m,\dotsc,m]}}

of
(\infty,2)
-categories, where the map on the right is natural in
\Delta\times\Delta
.

Proof of
4.3
.

Combining
4.20
and
4.3
, we deduce that there is a natural pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(N(\mathrm{sSet}^{+,\circ}))_{n,m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}([n],\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{lax}}([m]))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}([0],\textup{Cat}_{\infty})^{\times(n+1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}([0],\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{lax}}([m]))^{\times(n+1)}}

for
n,m\geq 0
.
Here the components of the right map are given by restricting to the objects of
[n]
. The bottom map is induced by the restriction of the degeneracy (pullback) functor

[m]\times-:\textup{Cat}_{\infty}\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/[m].

Since both
\mathrm{Cocart}^{\mathrm{lax}}([m])
and
\mathrm{Con}([m])
are full subcategories of
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/[m]
that contain the images of these degeneracies, we
deduce that, as a 2-fold Segal space,
\mathrm{sSet}^{+,\circ}
sits in levelwise pullback squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(N(\mathrm{sSet}^{+,\circ}))_{n,m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}([n],\mathrm{Con}([m]))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}([0],\textup{Cat}_{\infty})^{\times(n+1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}([0],\mathrm{Con}([m]))^{\times(n+1)}.}

This is also the defining feature of the vertical fragment of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
.
∎

5.
Example: the
\infty
-equipment of internal categories

Given an
\infty
-topos
\mathscr{E}
,
there is a notion of complete categorical objects, i.e.
internal (
\infty
-)categories
, in
\mathscr{E}
.
Moreover, one may develop a category theory for these internal categories, called
internal category theory
.
For presheaf
\infty
-toposes, this was firstly done in
[
BDG+16
]
via an ‘external’ approach,
where it is named
parametrized higher category theory
.
In a slightly more general setting than
\infty
-toposes, a first approach to internal
\infty
-category theory is due to Rasekh

[
Ras18
]
. In the setting of
\infty
-toposes, it is intensively studied by Martini and Wolf in (among others)
[
MW21
]
.
Classically, an introduction to category theory internal to a 1-topos can be found in
[
Joh02
]
.

As
\infty
-equipments have formal category theories (
6
) associated to them,
one might hope that there exists an
\infty
-equipment

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})

that recovers the category theory internal to the
\infty
-topos
\mathscr{E}
. We will
give a construction of such an
\infty
-equipment here,
which is a suitable generalization of the construction given in
4
,
so that we recover
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
if
\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{S}
.

5.1.
The double
\infty
-category of categorical objects

We commence by introducing an internal variant of Conduché fibrations. This is essentially a modified adaptation of the material in
[
Hau17
]

(applied to the cartesian case). Throughout, we fix an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
and assume the following three things about
\mathscr{C}
:

(1)

\mathscr{C}
has all finite limits,

(2)

\mathscr{C}
admits all geometric realizations (i.e. all colimits of
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
-shaped diagrams),

(3)

fibered products and geometric realizations in
\mathscr{C}
are compatible:
for any pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}

in
\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{C})
so that
D
is essentially constant, the induced square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}D\simeq D_{0}}

on geometric realizations is a pullback square as well.

By using that
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
is sifted, we see that it suffices to check assumption (3) for those pullback squares where
B
is essentially constant as well.
To wit, suppose that (3) holds in these special cases. Then for any general pullback square as in (3), we have a commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{([n],[m])\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}(B_{n}\times_{D_{0}}C_{m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}B\times_{D_{0}}\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}C,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

and the horizontal arrow is an equivalence since the diagonal
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
is final. Note that the top right colimit can be written
as an iterated colimit. With these observations, one can now verify that the right leg is an equivalence
under the assumptions (applying (3) twice for the special cases).

In particular, since
\infty
-toposes have all small (co)limits and universality of colimits, we see that conditions (1)-(3) are met if
\mathscr{C}
is an
\infty
-topos.

Definition 5.1
.

A
categorical
(n+1)
-module in
\mathscr{C}

is a functor

M:(\Delta/[n])^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}

which satisfies the
Segal condition
: for any map
f:[k]\rightarrow[n]
the Segal map

M(f)\rightarrow M(f|\{0,1\})\times_{M(f|\{1\})}\dotsb\times_{M(f|\{k-1\})}M(f|\{k-1,k\})

is an equivalence. We will write

\mathrm{Mod}_{n+1}(\mathscr{C})\subset\mathrm{Fun}((\Delta/[n])^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{C})

for the full subcategory spanned by these categorical
(n+1)
-modules.

Consider the cosimplicial object
C_{\bullet}
given by the composite

\Delta\xrightarrow{(-)^{\mathrm{op}}}\Delta\xrightarrow{\Delta/[\bullet]}\textup{Cat}_{\infty}\xrightarrow{(-)^{\mathrm{op}}}\textup{Cat}_{\infty}

where the middle functor acts by pushing forward.
Then we obtain a simplicial
\infty
-category

\mathrm{Fun}(C_{\bullet},\mathscr{C}):\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\widehat{\textup{Cat}}_{\infty}:[n]\mapsto\mathrm{Fun}((\Delta/[n])^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{C}).

One may readily verify that
sees that if
\alpha:[m]\rightarrow[n]
is a structure map, and
M
is a categorical
(n+1)
-module in
\mathscr{C}

then the
restriction of
M
along
\alpha

\alpha^{*}M:(\Delta/[m])^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{(\alpha^{\mathrm{op}}_{!})^{\mathrm{op}}}(\Delta/[n])^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{C},

is a categorical
(m+1)
-module. Hence, we obtain a subobject
\mathrm{Mod}_{\bullet+1}(\mathscr{C})
of
\mathrm{Fun}(C_{\bullet},\mathscr{C})

that is spanned by the categorical modules. However, this fails to be a double
\infty
-category in general.
The problem is that there are too many trimodules: not all are obtained by composing two bimodules. This can be remedied by restricting to the
trimodules that are obtained by composing two bimodules. To formulate this, we will need the following auxiliary shape category, defined by Haugseng:

Definition 5.2

(
[
Hau17
, Definition 4.13]
)
.

A map
f:[m]\rightarrow[n]
is called
cellular
if
f(i+1)\leq f(i)+1
for all
i\in[m]
, i.e. if
f
has convex image. The
full subcategory of
\Delta/[n]
spanned by these cellular maps is denoted by
\Lambda/[n]
.

We then have the following analogue of
4.2
:

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
M
be a categorical
(n+1)
-module in
\mathscr{C}
. Then, the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

M
is left Kan extended along the inclusion

(\Lambda/[n])^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow(\Delta/[n])^{\mathrm{op}},

(2)

for any map
\alpha:[2]\rightarrow[n]
, the restriction
\alpha^{*}M
is left Kan extended along the inclusion

(\Lambda/[2])^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow(\Delta/[2])^{\mathrm{op}},

(3)

for any map
\alpha:[2]\rightarrow[n]
, the canonical map

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}M([k+2]\xrightarrow{0\leq 1\dotsb\leq 1\leq 2}[2]\xrightarrow{\alpha}[n])\rightarrow M([1]\xrightarrow{0\leq 2}[2]\xrightarrow{\alpha}[n])

is an equivalence.

Remark 5.4
.

Note that condition (3) of
5.1
essentially asserts that if
M
is a trimodule in
\mathscr{C}
, then
M
is
left Kan extended along
(\Lambda/[2])^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow(\Delta/[2])^{\mathrm{op}}
if and only if
M(\{0\leq 2\})
is the two-sided bar construction
of
M(\{0\leq 1\})
and
M(\{1\leq 2\})
over
M(\{1\})
. For
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{S}
, the same bar construction formula makes an appearance in
[
AFR18
, Lemma 5.16(5)]
.

In order to prove the above proposition, we will make use of the following construction and observation (cf. Proposition 4.16 and Lemma 4.17 of
[
Hau17
]
):

Construction 5.5
.

Let
f:[m]\rightarrow[n]
be a map of posets. Then we will write
D(f)
for the set that measures the non-cellularity of
f
,
i.e.  it is given by the difference of the convex hull of
\mathrm{im}(f)
and
\mathrm{im}(f)
:

D(f):=\{f(0),f(0)+1,\dotsc,f(m)-1,f(m)\}\setminus\mathrm{im}(f).

We define a functor

\textstyle T_{f}:\prodop\displaylimits_{D(f)}\Delta\rightarrow f/(\Lambda/[n]):=f/(\Delta/[n])\times_{\Delta/[n]}\Lambda/[n]

that carries an indexed poset
[k_{\bullet}]
to the triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m+\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in D(f)}(k_{i}+1)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{[k_{\bullet}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{[k_{\bullet}]}}

where
f_{[k_{\bullet}]}
is the map

\textstyle f_{[k_{\bullet}]}:[1+\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in D(f|\{0,1\})}(k_{i}+1)]\cup_{[0]}\dotsb\cup_{[0]}[1+\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in D(f|\{m-1,m\})}(k_{i}+1)]\rightarrow[n]

so that
f^{j,j+1}_{[k_{\bullet}]}:=f_{[k_{\bullet}]}|[1+\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in D(f|\{j,j+1\})}(k_{i}+1)]
is given by

f^{j,j+1}_{[k_{\bullet}]}(x)=\begin{cases}f(j),&x=0\\
t,&\sumop\displaylimits_{i=f(j)+1}^{t-1}(k_{i}+1)<x\leq\sumop\displaylimits_{i=f(j)+1}^{t}(k_{i}+1)\\
f(j+1),&\text{if }x=1+\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in D(f|\{j,j+1\})}(k_{i}+1),\end{cases}

and the top map
\iota_{[k_{\bullet}]}
is the unique injective map that fits in the triangle. It is readily verified
that this construction is functorial, with the functoriality
of
T_{f}
being uniquely determined by pushing forward structure maps along the
canonical inclusions

\textstyle\rho^{i}_{[k_{\bullet}]}:[k_{i}]\rightarrow[1+\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in D(f|\{j,j+1\})}(k_{i}+1)]\rightarrow[m+\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in D(f)}(k_{i}+1)].

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
f:[m]\rightarrow[n]
be a map of posets. Then the functor
T_{f}
of
5.1
is final.

Proof.

In light of Quillen’s theorem A
[
Lur09a
, Theorem 4.1.3.1]
, it suffices to show that the category

\textstyle T_{f}/X:=(f/(\Lambda/[n]))/X\times_{f/(\Lambda/[n])}\prodop\displaylimits_{D(f)}\Delta

is weakly contractible for every
X\in f/(\Lambda/[n])
. Note that such
X
is given by a triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

so that
g
is cellular. It is readily verified that maps
\phi:T_{f}([k_{\bullet}])\rightarrow X
in
f/(\Lambda/[n])

are uniquely determined by the restrictions
\phi\rho^{i}_{[k_{\bullet}]}:[k_{i}]\rightarrow[l]
.
Since each restriction should have image in the (non-empty) fiber
g^{-1}(i)\subset[l]
,
we see that the choice of
\phi
so that

\phi\rho^{i}_{[k_{\bullet}]}:[k_{i}]\rightarrow[l]

is the unique injective map with image
g^{-1}(i)
, constitutes a final object of the lax slice
T_{f}/X
.
∎

Additionally, we will need the following two results:

Lemma 5.7
.

Suppose that
N:(\Lambda/[n])^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
satisfies the Segal condition as in
5.1
. Then
the left Kan extension
M:(\Delta/[n])^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
of
N
along
(\Lambda/[n])^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow(\Delta/[n])^{\mathrm{op}}
satisfies the Segal condition as well.

Proof.

Let
f:[m]\rightarrow[n]
be a map. Then, in view of
5.1
and the Segal condition
for
N
, we compute that

\displaystyle M(f)

\displaystyle=\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k_{\bullet}]\in\prodop\displaylimits_{D(f)}\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}N(f_{[k_{\bullet}]})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k_{\bullet}]\in\prodop\displaylimits_{D(f)}\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}N(f^{0,1}_{[k_{\bullet}]})\times_{N(f|\{1\})}\dotsb\times_{N(f|\{m-1\})}N(f^{m-1,m}_{[k_{\bullet}]})

Using the fact that we have a decomposition
D(f)=D(f|\{0,1\})\coprodop\displaylimits\dotsb\coprodop\displaylimits D(f|\{m-1,m\})

and compatibility of the fibered product with geometric realizations, we deduce that

M(f)\simeq M(f|\{0,1\})\times_{M(f|\{1\})}\dotsb\times_{M(f|\{m-1\})}M(f|\{m-1,m\}),

as desired.
∎

Lemma 5.8
.

Suppose that
M
is a categorical
(n+1)
-module in
\mathscr{C}
.
Let
f:[1]\rightarrow[n]
be a map of posets that
factors as

[1]\xrightarrow{g}[m]\xrightarrow{\alpha}[n]

so that
\alpha
is injective.
Then there is a canonical commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k_{\bullet}]\in\prodop\displaylimits_{D(g)}\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}M(\alpha g_{[k_{\bullet}]})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k_{\bullet}]\in\prodop\displaylimits_{D(f)}\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}M(f_{[k_{\bullet}]}),}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(f)}

and the top map is an equivalence as soon as
for all
0\leq j<m
, the comparison map

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k_{\bullet}]\in\prodop\displaylimits_{D(\alpha|\{j,j+1\})}\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}M(\alpha^{j,j+1}_{[k_{\bullet}]})\rightarrow M(\alpha|\{j,j+1\})

is an equivalence.

Proof.

Note that
\alpha
restricts to an inclusion
D(g)\rightarrow D(f)
and gives rise to a canonical
natural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prodop\displaylimits_{D(g)}\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta/[m])^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prodop\displaylimits_{D(f)}\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta/[n])^{\mathrm{op}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha_{!})^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{f}}

Hence, we obtain maps

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k_{\bullet}]\in\prodop\displaylimits_{D(g)}\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}M(\alpha g_{[k_{\alpha(\bullet)}]})\xleftarrow{\simeq}\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k_{\bullet}]\in\prodop\displaylimits_{D(f)}\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}M(\alpha g_{[k_{\alpha(\bullet)}]})\rightarrow\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k_{\bullet}]\in\prodop\displaylimits_{D(f)}\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}M(f_{[k_{\bullet}]}),

which are all compatible with the canonical projections to
M(f)
.
The first map is an equivalence as
\alpha^{*}
is final,
so that we may obtain the desired commutative triangle by picking an inverse.
To show the latter statement, we
suppose that the comparison maps for
\alpha
as stated in the lemma,
are equivalences. By 2-out-of-3, it suffices to show that the right arrow in the span displayed above, is an equivalence,
which readily follows from a similar computation as in
5.1
.
∎

Proof of
5.1
.

Let us first show that (2) and (3) are equivalent. A categorical module

M:(\Delta/[2])^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
is left Kan extended along the inclusion
(\Lambda/[2])^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow(\Delta/[2])^{\mathrm{op}}
if and only if
for any map
f:[k]\rightarrow[2]
, the canonical unit map

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{g\rightarrow f\in(\Lambda/[2])^{\mathrm{op}}/f}M(g)\rightarrow M(f)

is an equivalence. In light of the assumption on
M
and

5.1
, both sides satisfy the Segal condition when varying
f
.
Hence, one may reduce to checking that the above map is an equivalence for
k=1
.
The only non-cellular map is then given by
f=d_{1}
, so that after
rewriting the above colimit using
5.1
, one directly deduces that (2) and (3) are equivalent.

Next, we show that (1) implies (3). Suppose that
M

is a
(n+1)
-categorical module so that (1) holds. If
\alpha:[2]\rightarrow[n]

is an injective map, then the desired result
follows from applying
5.1
with
g=d_{1}
. In the case
that
\alpha
is not injective, the result follows from diagrammatic reasons.
To wit, we note that the simplicial object

S:\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow(\Delta/[n])^{\mathrm{op}}:[k]\mapsto([k+2]\xrightarrow{0\leq 1\dotsb\leq 1\leq 2}[2]\xrightarrow{\alpha}[n])

is (always) augmented by
\alpha d_{1}
, and this augmentation induces the comparison map

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}MS\rightarrow M(\alpha d_{1})

in question.
In the case that

\alpha
is not injective, one can verify that
S
admits a
splitting
(see
[
RV22
, Definition 2.3.13]
).
Hence, the composite
MS
admits a splitting and the comparison map will be an equivalence on account of
[
RV22
, Proposition 2.3.15]
.

Finally, suppose that
M
is a
(n+1)
-categorical module so that (3) holds. Then we will show that
M
satisfies (1).
To this end, we proceed by induction as follows.
For
f:[m]\rightarrow[n]
, let use define its
non-cellularity index

I(f)
by

I(f):=\max_{0\leq j<m}\left|D(f|\{j,j+1\})\right|.

We will show that for every map
f
, the comparison map

c(f):\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[k_{\bullet}]\in\prodop\displaylimits_{D(f)}\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}M(f_{[k_{\bullet}]})\rightarrow M(f)

is an equivalence by induction on
I(f)
. If
I(f)=0
, the statement is trivial.
Suppose now that the statement holds for all maps
f
so that
I(f)\leq i
, with
i\geq 0
. In order to show that the statement holds for
i+1
,
we may use
5.1
to reduce to showing the statement
for any map
f:[1]\rightarrow[n]
with
I(f)=i+1
. We may factor such a map
f
as

[1]\xrightarrow{d_{1}}[2]\xrightarrow{\alpha}[n]

so that
\alpha
is injective with
I(\alpha)=i
.
It follows now directly from the assumptions and
5.1
that
c(f)
is an equivalence as well.
∎

Definition 5.9
.

If
M
is a categorical
(n+1)
-module in
\mathscr{C}
that satisfies the equivalent
Conduché conditions
(1)-(3) of
5.1
,
then we say that
M
is a
Conduché module
. We write

\mathrm{Con}_{n+1}(\mathscr{C})\subset\mathrm{Mod}_{n+1}(\mathscr{C})

for the full subcategory spanned by
the Conduché modules.

Corollary 5.10
.

Every categorical bimodule in
\mathscr{C}
satisfies the Conduché condition.

Construction 5.11
.

In light of
5.1
, the Conduché property for categorical modules is preserved under
restriction along maps in
\Delta
. Consequently, we obtain a further subobject

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}(\mathscr{C})\subset\mathrm{Mod}_{\bullet+1}(\mathscr{C})\subset\mathrm{Fun}(C_{\bullet},\mathscr{C})

that is spanned by the Conduché modules in each level.

Now, a standard argument shows the following:

Proposition 5.12
.

The simplical
\infty
-category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}(\mathscr{C})
is a double
\infty
-category.

Proof.

Let
\mathrm{Con}_{(n+1)}^{\prime}(\mathscr{C})\subset\mathrm{Fun}((\Lambda/[n])^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{C})
be the full subcategory spanned
by those functors that satisfy the Segal condition as in
5.1
.
Then it readily
follows from characterization (1) of
5.1
and
5.1
that the fully faithful left Kan extension functor

i_{!}:\mathrm{Fun}((\Lambda/[n])^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}((\Delta/[n])^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{C})

along
i:(\Lambda/[n])^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow(\Delta/[n])^{\mathrm{op}}

restricts to an equivalence between
\mathrm{Con}_{n+1}^{\prime}(\mathscr{C})
and
\mathrm{Con}_{n+1}(\mathscr{C})
. Consequently,
we see that it suffices to check that the canonical functor

p:\mathrm{Con}^{\prime}_{n+1}(\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\mathrm{Con}_{1}(\mathscr{C})\times_{\textup{Cat}(\mathscr{C})}\dotsb\times_{\textup{Cat}(\mathscr{C})}\mathrm{Con}_{1}(\mathscr{C})

is an equivalence.

To this end, we note that this functor may be identified with the restriction of the left adjoint
that sits in the adjunction

j^{*}:\mathrm{Fun}((\Lambda/[n])^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{C})\rightleftarrows\mathrm{Fun}((\Lambda/[n])^{\mathrm{cell},\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{C}):j_{*}

associated to
right
Kan extending along the inclusion

j:(\Lambda/[n])^{\mathrm{cell},\mathrm{op}}\simeq(\Delta/[1])^{\mathrm{op}}\cup_{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\dotsb\cup_{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}(\Delta/[1])^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow(\Lambda/[n])^{\mathrm{op}}

of the full subcategory of those cellular maps
f:[m]\rightarrow[n]
so that
\mathrm{im}(f)\subset\{i,i+1\}
for some
i
.
Let us write
\mathrm{Con}^{\prime\prime}_{n+1}(\mathscr{C})\subset\mathrm{Fun}((\Lambda/[n])^{\mathrm{cell},\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{C})
for the full subcategory of
those functors that satisfy the Segal condition. Then
p
may be identified with the restriction of
j^{*}

to
\mathrm{Con}^{\prime}_{n+1}(\mathscr{C})
and
\mathrm{Con}^{\prime\prime}_{n+1}(\mathscr{C})
.
Since
j_{*}
is fully faithful, we thus see that
p
is an equivalence if the following holds:

(1)

the left adjoint
j_{*}
carries
\mathrm{Con}^{\prime\prime}_{n+1}(\mathscr{C})
into
\mathrm{Con}^{\prime}_{n+1}(\mathscr{C})
,

(2)

if
M\in\mathrm{Con}^{\prime}_{n+1}(\mathscr{C})
, then
M
is right Kan extended along
j
, i.e.  the unit

M\rightarrow j_{*}j^{*}M
is an equivalence,

in which case
j_{*}
restricts to an inverse of
p
.

Both (1) and (2) follow from the following computation. Let

M:(\Lambda/[n])^{\mathrm{cell},\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
be a functor and
f:[m]\rightarrow[n]
be a cellular map, then

j_{*}M(f)\simeq M(f|f^{-1}(\{\alpha,\alpha+1\}))\times_{M(f|f^{-1}(\{\alpha+1\}))}\dotsb\times_{M(f|f^{-1}(\{\omega-1\}))}M(f|f^{-1}(\{\omega-1,\omega\})),

where
\alpha:=\min(f)
and
\omega:=\max(f)
.
Consequently, if
M
satisfies the Segal condition then we get that

j_{*}M(f)\simeq M(f|\{0,1\})\times_{M(f|\{1\})}\dotsb\times_{M(f|\{m-1\})}M(f|\{m-1,m\}).\qed

Finally, we record the following functoriality of the construction above. It follows immediately from
5.1
and
5.1
.

Proposition 5.13
.

Suppose that
u:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
is a functor to an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{D}
that also has properties (1)-(3) stated at the start of
5.1
.
If
u
preserves pullbacks and geometric realizations, then post-composition with
u
gives
rise to a double functor
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}(u):\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}(\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}(\mathscr{D})
.

5.2.
Completeness conditions in
\infty
-toposes

Lurie has developed a theory of complete categorical objects with respect to a
distributor
in
[
Lur09b
]
. In particular,
if
\mathscr{E}
is an
\infty
-topos, this theory can be specialized to the trivial distributor
\mathscr{E}\subset\mathscr{E}
, yielding the notion of
complete categorical objects
in
\mathscr{E}
.
Since we do not want to recall the general theory of distributors, we give a simpler definition of these objects. It is shown by Haugseng in
[
Hau18
, Proposition 7.13]
that
this is equivalent to Lurie’s definition. Throughout, we fix an
\infty
-topos
\mathscr{E}
.

Definition 5.14
.

A categorical object in
\mathscr{C}
in
\mathscr{E}
is called
complete
if the composition

\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}\mathscr{E}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{E}}(X,-)}\mathscr{S}

defines a complete Segal space for every object
X\in\mathscr{E}
.
In this case, we will also call
\mathscr{C}
an
internal
(\infty
-)category in
\mathscr{C}
. We write

\textup{Cat}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})\subset\textup{Cat}(\mathscr{E})

for the full subcategory spanned by the complete objects.

More generally,a
(n+1)
-categorical module
M
in
\mathscr{E}
is said to be
complete
if for any vertex
0\leq i\leq n
, the restriction

(\{i\}\rightarrow[n])^{*}M
is a complete categorical object in
\mathscr{E}
. We write

\mathrm{CMod}_{n+1}(\mathscr{E})\subset\mathrm{Mod}_{n+1}(\mathscr{E}),\quad\mathrm{CCon}_{n+1}(\mathscr{E})\subset\mathrm{Con}_{n+1}(\mathscr{E}),

for the full subcategories of, respectively, complete categorical and Conduché modules in
\mathscr{E}
.

Remark 5.15
.

On account of
[
Mar21
, Proposition 3.2.7]
, a categorical object
\mathscr{C}
in
\mathscr{E}
is complete if and only if the
canonical map

\mathscr{C}_{0}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}_{0}\times_{\mathscr{C}(\{1\leq 3\})}\mathscr{C}([3])\times_{\mathscr{C}(\{0\leq 2\})}\mathscr{C}_{0}

is an equivalence.

Construction 5.16
.

By definition, the property of completeness for categorical modules is preserved under restricting along maps in
\Delta
. Moreover,
a categorical
(n+1)
-module is complete if and only if its restriction along each inert map is complete. Consequently,
the double
\infty
-category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}(\mathscr{E})
that we constructed in
5.1
, admits a sub double
\infty
-category

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})

that is levelwise spanned by the complete Conduché modules so that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})_{n}=\mathrm{CCon}_{n+1}(\mathscr{E})
.

Proposition 5.17
.

The double
\infty
-category
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
is an
\infty
-equipment.

Proof.

One may demonstrate in a way that is similar to the computation in the proof of
4.2
, that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
is vertically complete.
If
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
are internal categories in
\mathscr{E}
,
the vertical
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}))(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
is given by the object that is denoted by
\mathrm{MAP}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
in
[
Hau18
, Definition 7.12]
.

We will show that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
is an
\infty
-equipment by demonstrating that its source-target projection is a cartesian fibration. Note that we have a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CMod}_{2}(\mathscr{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{2}(\mathscr{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Cat}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})^{\times 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Cat}(\mathscr{E})^{\times 2},}

where the vertical arrows are the source-target projections of the double
\infty
-categories
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
and
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}(\mathscr{E})
. By definition,
this square is a pullback square. Thus, it suffices to show that source-target projection for
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}(\mathscr{E})
is a cartesian fibration.

Since
\textup{Cat}(\mathscr{E})\subset\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{E})
is a reflective subcategory, it has all pullbacks. Hence, it is enough to show that

\mathrm{Mod}_{2}(\mathscr{E})\rightarrow\textup{Cat}(\mathscr{E})^{\times 2}
admits a fully faithful right adjoint.
3
3

3

It is a general fact that any functor
p:E\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
whose target
\mathscr{C}
has all pullbacks, and that admits a fully faithful right adjoint, is a cartesian fibration.

By construction, this projection is the restriction of the functor

(d_{0}^{*},d_{1}^{*}):\mathrm{Fun}((\Delta/[1])^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{E})\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{E})^{\times 2},

which admits a fully faithful right adjoint
(-)\star(-)
given by right Kan extension along
(d_{0})_{!}\sqcup(d_{1})_{!}:\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\sqcup\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow(\Delta/[1])^{\mathrm{op}}
.
One may readily check that
X\star Y
is a categorical bimodule if
X
and
Y
are categorical objects in
\mathscr{E}
. Hence,
(-)\star(-)
restricts
to a fully faithful right adjoint for the source-target projection of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}(\mathscr{E})
.
∎

The
\infty
-equipments of internal
\infty
-categories are functorial with respect to the following class of maps:

Proposition 5.18
.

Suppose that
u:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{F}
is a functor to an
\infty
-topos
\mathscr{F}
. If

u
preserves pullbacks and geometric realizations,
then post-composition with
u

gives rise to a double functor

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(u):\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{F}).

Proof.

We have seen in
5.1
that
u
induces a double functor

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}(\mathscr{E})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}(\mathscr{F})
. Hence, it remains to check that post-composition with
u
preserves the completeness condition for categorical objects and this follows directly from
5.2
.
∎

We will use this observation in
[
Rui
]
, to demonstrate that a geometric morphism
f:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{F}
between
\infty
-toposes gives rise to
lax right adjoint

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{F})
between their associated
\infty
-equipments.

Finally, we will show that the construction above recovers the
\infty
-equipment of
\infty
-categories when

\mathscr{E}
is given by the
\infty
-topos of spaces, as promised.

Proposition 5.19
.

There is an equivalence

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{S})

between
\infty
-equipments.

To construct a double functor between the two equipments, we will need the following technicalities.
Let

\textup{PSh}\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{\infty}

be the large cartesian fibration that classifies the functor
\mathscr{C}\mapsto\textup{PSh}(\mathscr{C})
. If we write

\mathrm{RFib}\subset\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/[1]

for the full subcategory spanned by the right fibrations, then the functor

\mathrm{ev}_{1}:\mathrm{RFib}\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{\infty}.

given by evaluating in 1, is a cartesian fibration since right fibrations are stable under pullback. Gepner, Haugseng and Nikolaus show
that the unstraightening is (coherently) natural:

Proposition 5.20

(
[
GHN17
, Proposition A.31]
)
.

There is a map between cartesian fibrations

PSh
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{RFib},}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}

which may be identified with the unstraightening functor fiberwise. Consequently,
the top functor is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories.

We will write

\mathrm{RFib}(\mathscr{C})\subset\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/\mathscr{C}

for the full subcategory spanned by the right fibrations, or equivalently,
the fiber of
\mathrm{ev}_{1}
above
\mathscr{C}
. Note that we have a forgetful functor

\mathrm{fgt}:\mathrm{RFib}\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{\infty}
induced by evaluating at 0, which restricts to a forgetful functor
\mathrm{fgt}:\mathrm{RFib}(\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{\infty}
.
The following argument also appears in
[
GHN17
]
:

Lemma 5.21

(
[
GHN17
, Corollary 9.8]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a (small)
\infty
-category.
Then there is a pullback square of
\infty
-categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}([1],\textup{PSh}(\mathscr{C}))}
PSh
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{PSh}(\mathscr{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Cat}_{\infty},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{1}}

where the bottom arrow is the composition

\textup{PSh}(\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\mathrm{RFib}(\mathscr{C})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{fgt}}\textup{Cat}_{\infty},

so that the first arrow is given by the Grothendieck construction.

Proof.

By the straightening theorem and its naturality, it suffices to show that we have a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{RFib}(\mathscr{C}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{RFib}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{RFib}(\mathscr{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Cat}_{\infty}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{fgt}}

One readily checks that we have a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}([1],\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/\mathscr{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}([1],\textup{Cat}_{\infty})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Cat}_{\infty},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{fgt})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{fgt}}

which essentially follows from the equivalent presentation
\mathrm{Fun}([1],\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/\mathscr{C})\simeq\mathrm{Fun}([2],\textup{Cat}_{\infty})\times_{\mathrm{Fun}(\{2\},\textup{Cat}_{\infty})}\{\mathscr{C}\}
.
From this, one may deduce that there is a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{RFib}(\mathscr{C}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{RFib}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{RFib}(\mathscr{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Cat}_{\infty}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathrm{fgt})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{fgt}}

Here we used that if we have a commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}

where the slanted arrows are right fibrations, then
X\rightarrow Y
is a right fibration as well (working in the model of quasi-categories,
this may be verified by taking suitable replacements, cf.
[
Lur09a
, Corollary 2.2.3.14]
).
∎

Remark 5.22
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a (small)
\infty
-category, and
F\in\textup{PSh}(\mathscr{C})
a presheaf classified by a right fibration
p:Y\rightarrow X
.
Then
5.2
states that there is an equivalence

\textup{PSh}(Y)\simeq\textup{PSh}(\mathscr{C})/F

which is moreover natural in
F
. In particular, if
F
is represented by an object
x\in\mathscr{C}
, then we get that

\textup{PSh}(\mathscr{C}/x)\simeq\textup{PSh}(\mathscr{C})/F.

This has also been shown by Lurie using a different method
[
Lur09a
, Corollary 5.1.6.12]
.

Proof of
5.2
.

The restriction of the Yoneda embedding
\textup{Cat}_{\infty}\rightarrow\textup{PSh}(\Delta)

gives rise to a map between cartesian fibrations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}([1],\textup{Cat}_{\infty})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}([1],\textup{PSh}(\Delta))\times_{\textup{PSh}(\Delta)}\textup{Cat}_{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Cat}_{\infty},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{1}}

since it preserves (in particular) pullbacks.
We may now pull back along the twisted inclusion
\mathrm{tw}:\Delta\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{\infty}
(see
4.2
),
and pass to their straightenings, so that we obtain a natural transformation between
the simplicial
\infty
-categories that the resulting cartesian fibrations classify.
In light of
5.2
,
this is a natural transformation

y:S_{\bullet}\rightarrow\textup{PSh}(C_{\bullet}).

where
S_{\bullet}
is the simplicial
\infty
-category given by the composite

\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{tw}^{\mathrm{op}}}\textup{Cat}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/(-)}\widehat{\textup{Cat}}_{\infty}

Level-wise,
y_{n}
may be identified with the functor

\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/[n]\rightarrow\textup{PSh}(\Delta/[n])\simeq\textup{PSh}(\Delta)/[n]

given by restricting the Yoneda embedding along the inclusion
\Delta/[n]\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/[n]
, or
equivalently, given by slicing the restricted Yoneda embedding along
\Delta\rightarrow\textup{Cat}_{\infty}
over
[n]
.
In light of
[
JT07
]
, this factors through the equivalence

\textup{Cat}_{\infty}/[n]\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{CMod}_{n+1}(\mathscr{S})\simeq\textup{Cat}_{\infty}(\mathscr{S})/[n].

Thus
y
restricts to an equivalence

y:S_{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathrm{CMod}_{\bullet+1}(\mathscr{S})

between simplicial
\infty
-categories.
Now, we claim that
y
carries the Conduché fibrations to complete categorical modules that satisfy the Conduché condition.
It suffices to check this in the case that
n=2
, where the result follows from characterization (5) of
[
AFR18
, Lemma 5.16]
.
Note that there are no Conduché conditions in the levels
n=0,1
. Combining these observations and using the Segal conditions, we see that

y
restricts to the desired equivalence

y:\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{S}).\qed

6.
First notions of formal category theory

In this section, we introduce and study the basic notions of category theory internal to an
\infty
-equipment. The insight that one may encode
desirable universal properties via proarrows is central to developing formal category theories using the language of equipments. It is then the question of whether these proarrows are representable in the sense
that they are companions or conjoints. Throughout this section, we will fix an
\infty
-equipment
\mathscr{P}
.

6.1.
Fully faithfulness and adjoints

We will start by defining fully faithful arrows in an
\infty
-equipment, and
give a characterization of adjoint pairs in vertical fragments of
\infty
-equipments via
proarrows.

Definition 6.1
.

An arrow
f:x\rightarrow y
in
\mathscr{P}
is
fully faithful
if the horizontal identity 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is cartesian.

Proposition 6.2
.

Any equivalence
f:x\rightarrow y
in
\mathscr{P}
is fully faithful.

Proof.

Note that
f
is classified by a map
[1]\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{0}
, and we have to check
that

\mathrm{id}_{f}:[1]\rightarrow\mathscr{P}_{0}\xrightarrow{s_{0}}\mathscr{P}_{1}

is cartesian. This follows from the fact
that the first arrow factors over the point.
∎

Example 6.3
.

If
\mathscr{P}=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
, the above definition recovers the usual notion of fully faithful functors. To wit, suppose that
f:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
is a functor between

\infty
-categories. Then the identity 2-cell
\mathrm{id}_{f}
factors (essentially) uniquely as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{f}{\mathscr{D}}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

so that the bottom 2-cell is cartesian. From the perspective of profunctors, the top comparison 2-cell is given by the canonical map

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{C}}(-,-)\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{D}}(f-,f-)
in
\mathrm{Prof}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C})
. By the definition above,
f
is fully faithful in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
if and only if
this map is an equivalence in
\mathrm{Prof}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C})
, hence the result follows.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
f:x\rightarrow y
be an arrow in
\mathscr{P}
. If
f
is fully faithful,
then the pushforward map

f_{*}:\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(z,x)\rightarrow\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(z,y)

is fully faithful for all
z\in\mathscr{P}
.

Proof.

Using the companion embedding for
\mathscr{P}
, we obtain a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(z,x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(z,y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(z,x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(z,y).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({y}_{f})_{*}}

Since the vertical arrows are fully faithful, it suffices to show that the bottom functor is fully faithful.
In view of
2.3
and
3.1
, this bottom functor is left adjoint to

({}_{f}{y})_{*}
. Thus, this functor is fully faithful if (and only if) the unit for this adjunction is an equivalence.
But this unit is induced by the pasting
\eta
of the proof of
3.1
.
This pasting is an equivalence in
\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,x)
since
f
is fully faithful,
hence
({y}_{f})_{*}
must be fully faithful.
∎

Proposition 6.5
.

Suppose
that
f:x\rightarrow y
and
g:y\rightarrow x
are arrows in
\mathscr{P}
. Then
(f,g)
is an adjunction pair in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
if and only if
there exists an equivalence
{}_{f}{y}\simeq{x}_{g}
in
\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(y,x)
.

Proof.

The companion embedding
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})
is a locally fully faithful map between 2-fold Segal spaces,
thus
(f,g)
is an adjunction pair
in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
if and only if
({y}_{f},{x}_{g})
is an adjunction pair in
\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})
. In light of
3.1
,
the pair
({y}_{f},{}_{f}{y})
is always
an adjunction pair in
\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})
. Thus by the essential uniqueness of adjoints
2.3
, we deduce that
f
and
g
are adjoint if and only if
{}_{f}{y}\simeq{x}_{g}
in
\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(y,x)
.
∎

Corollary 6.6
.

An arrow
f:x\rightarrow y
in
\mathscr{P}
admits a right adjoint in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
if and only if
{}_{f}{y}
is a companion. Similarly,
an arrow
g:y\rightarrow x
in
\mathscr{P}
admits a left adjoint in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
if and only if
{x}_{g}
is a conjoint.

Remark 6.7
.

If we consider
\mathscr{P}=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
and view proarrows as correspondences, this recovers the fact that
a functor
f:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
is a left adjoint if and only if the cocartesian fibration that classifies

f
is also a cartesian fibration.

Proposition 6.8
.

Suppose that
f:x\rightarrow y
and
g:y\rightarrow x
are arrows in
\mathscr{P}
so that
(f,g)
is an adjunction
in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
. Then
f
is fully faithful if and only if the unit of this adjunction is an equivalence. Similarly,

g
is fully faithful if the counit is an equivalence.

Proof.

We only demonstrate the first assertion as the statement for counits is shown similarly. It follows from
6.1
that
{x}_{g}
is the conjoint of
f
.
Moreover, the unit for the adjunction
(f,g)
corresponds to the pasting

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x}_{g}}

under the companion embedding (see the proof of
3.1
). This unit fits in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x}_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

which must paste to the horizontal identity 2-cell of
f
on account of the companion and conjoint identities. Also, the pasting of the bottom row is cartesian.
Consequently, this shows that the unit
x\rightarrow{x}_{gf}
may be identified with the (essentially) unique factorization
x\rightarrow{}_{f}{y}_{f}
induced by the identity 2-cell
\mathrm{id}_{f}
.
Combining this observation with the fact that the companion embedding is locally fully faithful, we obtain the desired result.
∎

6.2.
Weighted colimits and limits

Equipments support an internal notion of
weighted
colimits and limits.
This concept finds its roots in enriched category theory as the suitable generalization of ordinary (co)limits and was introduced by Borceux and Kelly
[
BK75
]
. In the setting of
\infty
-categories, weighted (co)limits were introduced by Gepner, Haugseng and Nikolaus
[
GHN17
]
, and Rovelli
[
Rov21
]
, both using a different approach. It was later shown by Haugseng
[
Hau22
, Corollary 4.5]
that
the two definitions agree. In
[
Hin21
]
, Hinich introduced a notion of weighted (co)limits for enriched
\infty
-categories.

We will need the following terminology:

Definition 6.9
.

Suppose that
f:i\rightarrow x
is an arrow in
\mathscr{P}
. Then there are
the following notions of
proarrows of cones
:

•

If
W:j\rightarrow i
is a proarrow of
\mathscr{P}
, then a proarrow
C:x\rightarrow j
that represents the presheaf

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,i)}(W_{*}(-),{}_{f}{x}):\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,j)^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}

is called a
proarrow of
W
-cones under
f
.

•

If
W:i\rightarrow j
is a proarrow of
\mathscr{P}
, then a proarrow
C:j\rightarrow x
that represents the presheaf

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(i,x)}(W^{*}(-),{x}_{f}):\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(j,x)^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}

is called a
proarrow of
W
-cones over
f
.

In this context, the proarrows
W
that appear above are also called
weights
.

Remark 6.10
.

Note that the choice of conjoint and companion of
f
in the definition above do not matter, as they are unique
up to suitable equivalence. This also follows from the following observation. If we have a cartesian 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

in
\mathscr{P}
,
then we obtain a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(a,b)}(-,G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{1}}(-,F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{(f,g)\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{0}}(c,d)\times\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{P}_{0}}(a,b)}

of presheaves
(\mathscr{P}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{P}_{0}^{\times 2}}\{(a,b)\})^{\mathrm{op}}\simeq\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(a,b)^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
.

Remark 6.11
.

If
\mathscr{P}
is horizontally closed then it admits all proarrows of cones.

Example 6.12
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
and let
f:I\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
be a functor. We may consider the
coconical weight

W:[0]\rightarrow I
, which has the defining property
that it is a conjoint. From the viewpoint of profunctors,

W
is given by the constant presheaf

I^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}:i\mapsto\ast.

In light of
4.2
, the proarrow of
W
-cones may be computed pointwise by an end:

\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}:c\mapsto\intop\nolimits_{i\in I}\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{C}}(f(i),c)\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Fun}(I,\mathscr{C})}(f,\mathrm{const}_{c}).

This retrieves the usual presheaf that carries
c\in\mathscr{C}
to
the space of cones under
f
with apex
c
.

We may also verify this from the viewpoint of two-sided discrete fibrations.
In light of Stevenson’s result discussed in
4.2
, the two-sided discrete fibration
{}^{W}{({}_{f}{\mathscr{C}})}

of
W
-cones under
f
fits in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(I,{}_{f}{\mathscr{C}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(I\times[1],\mathscr{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(I,\mathscr{C})\times\mathrm{Fun}(I,I)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(I,\mathscr{C})\times\mathrm{Fun}(I,\mathscr{C}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{W}{({}_{f}{\mathscr{C}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{ev}_{1},\mathrm{ev}_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\times f_{*}}

where both squares are pullback squares. The left bottom functor is given by the product of the diagonal functor
and
the constant map that selects the identity functor on
I
. Since the outer square is again a pullback square,
we deduce that the proarrow of
W
-cones under
f
is given by the usual projection
f/\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
.

With the terminology of proarrows of cones in place, may now define a notion of (co)limits:

Definition 6.13
.

Let
f:i\rightarrow x
be an arrow in
\mathscr{P}
.
Then the
colimit of
f
weighted by a proarrow

W:j\rightarrow i
is an arrow
g:j\rightarrow x
such that its conjoint

{}_{g}{x}
is a proarrow of
W
-cones under
f
.

Similarly, the
limit of
f
weighted by a proarrow

W:i\rightarrow j
is an arrow
g:j\rightarrow x
such that
the companion
{x}_{g}
is a proarrow of
W
-cones over
f
.

Example 6.14
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
. Let
f:I\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
be a functor between
\infty
-categories. In light of
6.2
,
we see that the notion of colimits of
f
weighted by the coconical weight
W:[0]\rightarrow I
, coincides with the ordinary notion of colimits of
f
.

More generally, suppose that
W:[0]\rightarrow I
is a weight, not necessarily the conical weight. Viewed as a profunctor,
such a weight is given by a presheaf
W:I^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
. From this perspective, the proarrow
C
of
W
-cones under
f
is then given by the end formula

C:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}:c\mapsto\intop\nolimits_{i\in I}\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{S}}(W(i),\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{C}}(f(i),c)).

An object
x\in\mathscr{C}
, equivalently, a functor
x:[0]\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
, is the
W
-weighted colimit of
f
if and only if
C
is represented by
x
. This coincides with the notion of weighted colimits of
[
GHN17
]
(precisely, the definition given in
[
Hau22
, Section 4]
).

From the perspective of two-sided discrete fibrations,
W
is given by a right fibration
p:I^{\prime}\rightarrow I
. A similar computation as in
6.2
,
shows that the proarrow
C
(as a two-sided discrete fibration) is given by the left fibration

fp/\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}.

Hence, an object
x\in\mathscr{C}
is the
W
-weighted colimit of
f
if and only if it is the (ordinary) colimit of the diagram
fp:I^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
.
In view of
[
Rov21
, Theorem 2.37]
, this coincides with Rovelli’s definition of weighted colimits. In particular, we recover
[
Hau22
, Theorem 1.2]
and the fact that the definitions of weighted colimits by Rovelli and Gepner-Haugseng-Nikolaus agree
[
Hau22
, Corollary 4.5]
.

The analogous statements for weighted limits are true as well.

Proposition 6.15
.

Suppose that we have an adjunction

u:x\rightleftarrows y:v

in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
. Let
f:i\rightarrow x
be an arrow of
\mathscr{P}
. Then we have the following preservations of proarrows of cones:

(1)

If
C:x\rightarrow j
is a proarrow of cones under
f
weighted by
W:j\rightarrow i
, then
{C}^{u}
is
a proarrow of
W
-cones under
uf
.

(2)

Similarly, if
C:j\rightarrow x
is a proarrow of cones over
f
weighted by
W:i\rightarrow j
,
then
{}_{v}{C}
is a proarrow
of
W
-cones over
vf
.

Proof.

We prove the first statement since the proof of the latter is similar. On account of
6.1
,
we may identify

{C}^{u}\simeq C\circ{}_{u}{y}\simeq C\circ{x}_{v}\simeq{C}_{v}
.
Hence, we have an equivalence

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(y,j)}(F,{C}^{u})\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,j)}({F}^{v},C).

natural in proarrows
F:y\rightarrow j
(see
3.2
).
Since
C
is a proarrow of
W
-cones under
f
, we deduce that

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(y,j)}(F,{C}^{u})\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,i)}(W\circ{F}^{v},{}_{f}{x})\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(y,i)}(W\circ F,{}_{f}{x}_{v}).

Note that
{}_{f}{x}_{v}\simeq{}_{f}{x}{x}_{v}\simeq{}_{f}{x}{}_{u}{y}\simeq{}_{uf}{x}
.
Thus we obtain the desired equivalence

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(y,j)}(F,{C}^{u})\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(y,i)}(W\circ F,{}_{uf}{x}).\qed

Corollary 6.16
.

Suppose that
(u,v)
is an adjunction in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
. Then
u
preserves all weighted colimits and
v
preserves all weighted limits.

Proposition 6.17
.

Let
x
be an object of
\mathscr{P}
and suppose that
W:j\rightarrow i
is a weight. Consider
the full subcategory

A\subset\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(i,x)

spanned by those arrows
that admit a
W
-weighted colimit. Then there exists a functor

{\mathop{colim}\displaylimits}^{W}:A\rightarrow\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(j,x)

that carries an arrow to its
W
-weighted colimit.

Proof.

Let us consider the composite functor

C:\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(i,x)^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,i)\rightarrow\textup{PSh}(\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,i))\xrightarrow{(W_{*})^{*}}\textup{PSh}(\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,j)),

where the first arrow is the conjoint embedding and the second arrow is given by the Yoneda embedding, so that

C(f)=\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,i)}(W_{*}(-),{}_{f}{x}).

Note that there is a similarly defined fully faithful functor

i:\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(j,x)^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,j)\rightarrow\textup{PSh}(\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,j)),

given by the conjoint embedding followed by the Yoneda embedding, so that

i(g)=\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,j)}(-,{}_{g}{x}).

The defining feature of
A
is that it is the largest subcategory so the essential image of
R|A^{\mathrm{op}}
coincides with the essential image
of
i
. Consequently,
R
factors through
i
so that we obtain the desired functor
{\mathop{colim}\displaylimits}^{W}
(after applying the involution
(-)^{\mathrm{op}}
).
∎

Example 6.18
.

Consider the
\infty
-equipment
\widehat{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}}_{\infty}
of large
\infty
-categories (see
4.2
). The proarrows that lie in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}\subset\widehat{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}}_{\infty}
, will be called

small proarrows
.
If
\mathscr{C}
is a cocomplete
\infty
-category, then it admits all colimits
weighted by these small proarrows. To wit, suppose that
f:I\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
is a functor, and let
W:J\rightarrow I
be a small proarrow.
Then both
I
and
J
are small
\infty
-categories, and as a profunctor,
W
is given by a functor
I^{\mathrm{op}}\times J\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
. Since
\mathscr{C}
is cocomplete,
it is tensored over spaces, i.e. there exists a functor

-\otimes-:\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}

so that

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{C}}(K\otimes x,y)\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{S}}(K,\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{C}}(x,y)),

naturally for all
x,y\in\mathscr{C}
and (small) spaces
K
. Now, one readily verifies
that the
W
-weighted colimit
g:J\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
of
f
exists, and is given by the coend

g(j)=\intop\nolimits^{i\in I}W(i,j)\otimes f(i).

Conversely,
6.2
implies that any
\infty
-category that admits all colimit weighted by small proarrows, is cocomplete. Again,
there are analogous statements for complete
\infty
-categories and weighted limits.

6.3.
Pointwise Kan extensions

We have the following familiar terminology for (co)limits weighted by conjoints and companions:

Definition 6.19
.

Let
f:i\rightarrow x
and
w:i\rightarrow j
be arrows of
\mathscr{P}
.
Then a colimit of
f
weighted by the conjoint
{}_{w}{j}
is called the
(pointwise) left Kan extension

of the arrow
f
along
w
.

Similarly, a limit of
f
weighted by the companion

{j}_{w}
is called a
(pointwise) right Kan extension
of
f
along
w
.

Construction 6.20
.

Suppose that
g:j\rightarrow x
is the left Kan extension of
f:i\rightarrow x
along
an arrow
w:i\rightarrow j
of
\mathscr{P}
. Then there exists a canonical 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
. Namely, the conjoint
{}_{gw}{x}
represents the presheaf

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,i)}({}_{w}{j}_{w}\circ(-),{}_{f}{x}):\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,i)^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S},

thus, the canonical comparison 2-cell
\mathrm{id}_{i}\rightarrow{}_{w}{j}_{w}
gives rise to 2-cell

{}_{gw}{x}\rightarrow{}_{f}{x}

and by locally fully faithfulness of the conjoint embedding, this yields a 2-cell

f\rightarrow gw
.

There is a similar story for right Kan extensions. Namely, if
g
is now the right Kan extension
of
f
along
w
, then there exists a canonical 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
which is constructed in the same manner.

Corollary 6.21
.

Suppose that
w:i\rightarrow j
is a fully faithful arrow in
\mathscr{P}
and let
f:i\rightarrow x
be an arrow of
\mathscr{P}
.
If
g
is the left Kan extension of
f
along
w
, then the canonical 2-cell
f\rightarrow gw
is invertible. Similarly,
if
g
is the right Kan extension of
f
along
w
, then the canonical 2-cell
gw\rightarrow f
is invertible.

The following may readily be deduced from
6.2
:

Proposition 6.22
.

Suppose that
w:i\rightarrow j
is an arrow of
\mathscr{P}
. Let
x
be an object of
\mathscr{P}
and write

A\subset\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(i,x)
the full subcategory spanned by those arrows that admit
a left Kan extension along
w
. Then there exists a partial left adjoint

w_{!}:A\rightarrow\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(j,x)

to the restriction functor

w^{*}:\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(j,x)\rightarrow\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(i,x)

that carries each arrow in
A
to its left Kan extension. Moreover, the associated unit of this (partial) adjunction
is the one constructed in
6.3
. There is a similar statement for right Kan extensions.

Remark 6.23
.

Any
(\infty,2)
-category
\mathscr{X}
supports a local notion of Kan extensions. Namely, if
f:i\rightarrow x
and
w:i\rightarrow j
are
arrows of
\mathscr{X}
, then the
weak left Kan extension
of
f
along
w

is any arrow that represents the presheaf

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{X}(i,x)}(f,w^{*}(-)):\mathscr{X}(j,x)\rightarrow\mathscr{S}.

This notion was explored earlier in the introduction of this article. In light of
6.3
, any (pointwise) Kan extension is a weak Kan extension. However,
the reverse implication fails in general.

We conclude this subsection by studying
Beck-Chevalley conditions
for these pointwise Kan extensions.
Central to this, is the following definition (cf.
[
RV22
, Definition 9.2.2]
):

Definition 6.24
.

A lax commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
is said to be
exact
if the induced comparison 2-cell

{}^{p}{i}^{w}\rightarrow{}_{v}{l}_{q}

is an equivalence in
\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(j,k)
.

Remark 6.25
.

This terminology dates back to Hilton
[
Hil66
]
, and was originally introduced in the context
of abelian categories. It was later
further categorified by Guitart
[
Gui80
]
. In Section 4 of loc. cit., Guitart studies similar phenomena in the Yoneda structures of Street and Walters.

Proposition 6.26
.

Suppose that we have a lax commutative square as in
6.3
. Let

f:k\rightarrow x
be an arrow of
\mathscr{P}
that admits a left Kan extension
v_{!}f:l\rightarrow x
along
v
.
Then the following is true:

(1)

if
w_{!}(fp):j\rightarrow x
is a left Kan extension along of
fp
along
w
, then there exists a canonical comparison 2-cell

w_{!}(fp)\rightarrow(v_{!}f)q,

(2)

and if the square is exact, then the left Kan extension
w_{!}(fp)
exists and this comparison 2-cell is an equivalence.

Proof.

The existence of the comparison 2-cell of (1) follows from the universal property of weak Kan extensions (cf.
6.3
).
This can also be constructed as follows. Note that the conjoint of
(v_{!}f)q
represents the presheaf

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,k)}({}_{v}{l}\circ{}^{q}{(-)},{}_{f}{x})\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,k)}({}_{v}{l}_{q}\circ(-),{}_{f}{x}).

Hence, the canonical 2-cell
{}^{p}{i}^{w}\rightarrow{}_{v}{l}_{q}
gives rise to a map between presheaves

\displaystyle\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,j)}(-,{}_{(v_{!}f)q}{x})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,k)}({}^{p}{i}^{w}\circ(-),{}_{f}{x})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(x,i)}({}_{w}{j}\circ(-),{}_{fp}{x}).

In the situation of (1), the presheaf on the right is represented by the conjoint of
w_{!}(fp)
. Hence, under the Yoneda and conjoint embeddings,
this would give the comparison 2-cell. Moreover, (2) follows from these observations as well.
∎

Corollary 6.27
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is an
\infty
-equipment
with an exact square as in
6.3
. Let
x
be an object of
\mathscr{P}
, and suppose
that each arrow
i\rightarrow x
and
j\rightarrow x
in
\mathscr{P}
admit left Kan extensions
along
w
and
v
respectively so that
the Kan extension functors
w_{!}
and
v_{!}
of
6.3
exist. Then
the mate transformation

w_{!}p^{*}\rightarrow w_{!}p^{*}v^{*}v_{!}\rightarrow w_{!}w^{*}q^{*}v_{!}\rightarrow q^{*}v_{!}

is an equivalence in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(k,x)
.

Example 6.28
.

Consider a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

of
\infty
-categories. If
q
is smooth (see
[
Lur09a
, Definition 4.1.2.9]
) or
v
is proper (see
[
Cis19
, Definition 4.4.1]
),
then this square is exact in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
. This may be deduced from
[
Cis19
, Proposition 6.4.3]

Example 6.29
.

A map
f:x\rightarrow y
in
\mathscr{P}
is fully faithful if and only if the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is exact.

6.4.
Initiality and finality

Under
an extra assumption on the
\infty
-equipment
\mathscr{P}
, we may define a notion of initial and final arrows in
\mathscr{P}
.

Definition 6.30
.

We call
\mathscr{P}

pointed
if its vertical fragment
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
has a final object
\ast
, in the sense
that the (unique) map
