\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(x,\ast)\rightarrow[0]

is an equivalence for all
x\in\mathscr{P}
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is pointed and
i
is an object of
\mathscr{P}
. Then the
coconical weight

{\Delta}^{i}
and the
conical weight

{}^{i}{\Delta}
are, respectively, the conjoint and companion
of the essentially unique arrow
i\rightarrow\ast
. If the colimit (resp. limit) of a diagram
f:i\rightarrow x
weighted by the coconical weight (resp. conical weight) exists, then
these are called
conical
, and we denote them by
\mathop{colim}\displaylimits f
(resp.
\lim f
).

Remark 6.31
.

In the context of the definition above, note that any conjoint in
\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(\ast,i)
or any companion in

\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(i,\ast)
is a coconical or conical weight, respectively.

Example 6.32
.

The
\infty
-equipment
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
is pointed. More generally, if
\mathscr{E}
is an
\infty
-topos then

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
is pointed.

Proposition 6.33
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is pointed and let
f:i\rightarrow j
be an arrow in
\mathscr{P}
. The following statements are equivalent:

(1)

there exists an equivalence
{}^{f}{\Delta}^{i}\simeq{\Delta}^{j}
in
\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(\ast,j)
,

(2)

the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ast}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ast}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is exact.

Dually, the following statements are equivalent:

(1’)

there exists an equivalence
{}^{i}{\Delta}^{f}\simeq{}^{j}{\Delta}
in
\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(j,\ast)
,

(2’)

the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ast}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ast}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is exact.

Proof.

Clearly, (2) implies (1). Conversely, if (1) holds, then any map

{}^{f}{\Delta}^{i}\rightarrow{\Delta}^{j}

is essentially unique since
\ast
is final in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
and the conjoint embedding is locally fully faithful.
Thus (1) implies (2). The demonstration that (1’) and (2’) are equivalent is similar.
∎

Definition 6.34
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is pointed. Let
f:i\rightarrow j
be an arrow in
\mathscr{P}
.
Then
f
is called
final
if one of the equivalent conditions (1) or (2) of
6.4
holds. Dually,

f
is called
initial
if condition (1’) or (2’) of
6.4
holds.

Using
6.3
, we now immediately obtain the following result:

Proposition 6.35
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is pointed.
Let
f:i\rightarrow j
and
g:j\rightarrow x
be arrows in
\mathscr{P}
.
Suppose that
f
is final, then the colimit of
g
exists if and only if the colimit of
gf
exists, and in this case, the
canonical map

\mathop{colim}\displaylimits gf\rightarrow\mathop{colim}\displaylimits g

is an equivalence.

Dually, if
f
is initial, then the limit of
g
exists if and only if the limit of
gf
exists
and in this case, the canonical map

\lim g\rightarrow\lim gf

is an equivalence.

Like in the case of final and initial functors between
\infty
-categories (
[
Lur09a
, Proposition 4.1.1.3]
),
final and initial arrows in a pointed
\infty
-equipment have a cancellation property:

Proposition 6.36
.

Suppose that
f:i\rightarrow j
and
g:j\rightarrow k
are arrows in a pointed
\infty
-equipment
\mathscr{P}
.
If
f
is final (resp. initial), then the composite
gf
is final (resp. initial) if and only if
g
is final (resp. initial).

Proof.

If
f
is final, we have

{}^{gf}{\Delta}^{i}\simeq{}^{g}{({}^{f}{\Delta}^{i})}\simeq{}^{g}{\Delta}^{j}.

From this computation, the statement for finality follows immediately. The dual statement for initiality is shown similarly.
∎

We can also give an analogue of
Quillen’s theorem A
, for so-called
strongly pointed equipments
:

Definition 6.37
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is pointed. Then
\mathscr{P}
is called

strongly pointed
if
for any proarrow
F:x\rightarrow y
, the following two statements hold:

(1)

F
is a companion if and only if for every arrow
p:\ast\rightarrow x
, the proarrow
{F}_{p}
is a companion,

(2)

F
is a conjoint if and only if for every arrow
p:\ast\rightarrow x
, the proarrow
{}_{p}{F}
is a conjoint.

Example 6.38
.

The
\infty
-equipment
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
is strongly pointed. This follows from the Yoneda lemma.
Indeed, from the perspective of profunctors, a proarrow
F:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
is a companion
if and only if the associated functor
\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\textup{PSh}(\mathscr{D})
factors through the Yoneda embedding. Since the Yoneda embedding
is fully faithful, one just has to check that for every object
p\in\mathscr{C}
, the evaluation in
p
(which corresponds to
{F}_{p}
) is representable.
A similar argument shows that condition (2) also holds.

This is not true in general: if
\mathscr{E}
is an arbitrary
\infty
-topos, then

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
will
virtually never
be strongly pointed.

Proposition 6.39
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is strongly pointed.
Then an arrow
f:i\rightarrow j
in
\mathscr{P}
is final
if and only if for any
p:\ast\rightarrow j
, we have

{}_{p}{({}^{f}{\Delta}^{i})}\simeq\ast

in
\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})(\ast,\ast)
.

Proof.

This follows directly from
6.4
and the definition of strongly pointed equipments.
∎

Example 6.40
.

Take
\mathscr{P}=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
.
Suppose that
f:I\rightarrow J
is a functor between
\infty
-categories. From the perspective of two-sided discrete fibrations, the coconical weight
W:={\Delta}^{I}

is given by the two-sided discrete fibration

[0]\leftarrow I=I.

Hence,
{}^{g}{W}={J}_{g}\circ W
corresponds to the two-sided discrete fibration

[0]\leftarrow I\times_{\mathrm{Fun}(\{1\},J)}\mathrm{Fun}([1],J)\rightarrow J.

Thus in light of the proposition above,
f
is final if and only if

{}_{p}{({}^{g}{W})}\simeq(I\times_{J}j/J)[(I\times_{J}j/J)^{-1}]\simeq\ast

in
\mathrm{Hor}(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty})([0],[0])\simeq\mathrm{DFib}([0],[0])\simeq\mathscr{S}
for any
j\in J
. This is precisely the content of Quillen’s theorem A for
\infty
-categories (in this full generality, originally due to Joyal).

*
